48,2026-11-24,"Jensen Huang returns to lead the most consequential company powering the AI revolution. We take a look at winners and losers from the scandal.
Save
Share
Less than two weeks after Jensen Huang, the leather-jacket-wearing face of the AI revolution, scandalously fell from the throne of the modern AI industry, he has reclaimed his leadership of the world’s most valuable semiconductor company, Nvidia.
It took a war room of Silicon Valley insiders lobbying the White House for US President Donald Trump to crack the whip on Monday and force chip giant Nvidia’s board to reinstate Huang, 63, who fell from grace following revelations of extramarital affairs, including with suspected Chinese spy Alice Huang, an executive and controlling shareholder of Megaspeed, an Nvidia client company that has since been blacklisted in the US.




What happens at Nvidia extends well beyond the California-based company.
Share values on the US stock market have become concentrated in a small group of increasingly valuable tech giants, which account for more than 60 percent of the Nasdaq-100. Tech and AI-linked companies together represent around half the S&P 500’s market value.
Huang’s return to Nvidia on Monday triggered a 30 percent rise in Nvidia shares, but has raised several questions about the reach of the Trump administration’s dictates as investigations into the company and its CEO continue. It has also heightened worries about the future of AI development amid Washington’s deepening rivalry with Beijing.
We take a look at major winners and losers from this whole scandal.
There are many.
The sacked CEO, who stunningly returned to applause from Nvidia employees on Monday, had left the board, which last week said it would not reinstate him.
From washing dishes at a Denny’s in the 1980s to building a $4 trillion technology giant, Huang led Nvidia for nearly 33 years and became one of Silicon Valley’s defining entrepreneurial stories.
He now joins a number of “cult-style” leaders in Silicon Valley, including Meta’s Mark Zuckerberg and SpaceX’s Elon Musk, who appear too “big” to fire.
Allegations that Huang’s affair with Alice Huang (no relation), a businesswoman suspected of having links to Chinese intelligence, may have exposed restricted chip technology to Beijing were ultimately not enough to keep him out of the CEO’s office.
During the 11 days he was out of the office, media reports detailed at least two more extramarital affairs with employees at Nvidia, and revealed how he toxically berated his subordinates.
“If Jensen comes back, I’m not doing any HR trainings ever again,” an Nvidia employee wrote on an internal platform, reported Wired.
Lloyd Blankfein, the former CEO of investment bank Goldman Sachs, wrote on X: “Guess you can come back from the graveyard if you’re really, really, really indispensable.”
For his part, President Trump argued that he just returned from a trip to China – where he attended the APEC summit and met with Chinese President Xi Jinping, “So I guess I’m involved also.”
Speaking with CNBC, Trump added, “Every time you have a Chinese woman that’s good-looking or beautiful, they say she’s a spy.” However, he said, “I don’t believe that’s true,” in the case of Huang. He did not comment on US intel reports he received.
The chip giant holds the key to the modern AI computing industry; its customers include Microsoft, Google, Amazon and Meta, who rely on Nvidia hardware to build and operate their AI data centres.
Nvidia’s shares fell nearly 28 percent between November 11, when the board accepted Huang’s resignation, and November 20, amid mounting uncertainty over its future leadership.
By the end of Monday, November 23, Nvidia recovered approximately $956bn in market value, rising from its November 20 low of $3.04 trillion to $3.98 trillion, and remaining the world’s most valuable semiconductor company.
Among those shareholders is US President Trump. A review of Trump’s financial disclosures, first reported by The Washington Post, found that he held between $13m and $62m in Nvidia stock at the end of August, up from about $1.3m at the end of 2024.
The AI industry at large felt the ripples from Huang’s exit from Nvidia, and his return is also boosting companies across the chip giant’s global supply chain, which are hoping for continuity in the product roadmap, manufacturing orders and investment plans.
Shares in Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company (TSMC), which manufactures Nvidia’s advanced AI processors, surged 9.9 percent on Tuesday, hitting the company’s daily trading limit and adding $158bn to its market value in a single session.
Shares in Foxconn, which assembles AI servers powered by Nvidia chips, also rose nearly 10 percent.
Shares in South Korean memory-chip makers SK Hynix and Samsung Electronics gained 6.7 percent and 6.6 percent, respectively.
Japanese chip-testing equipment maker Advantest shares jumped 12.5 percent.
On Tuesday, major Asian stock indexes in Seoul, Tokyo, and Taipei all rose.
Huang has spent years lobbying in Washington against restrictions to tech exports, arguing that halting sales will surrender a strategically important market to domestic Chinese competitors.
That puts him at odds with US lawmakers who view access to Nvidia’s advanced chips as a national security risk, particularly given their potential military applications.
The US first introduced export controls on the chips in 2022 and has subsequently tightened China’s access to them.
In one exception, following a summit with Chinese President Xi Jinping last year, Trump said he would allow H200 chip sales to approved Chinese customers. The US ‌eased ⁠its licensing policy for the powerful H200 in January, allowing export applications to be reviewed individually subject to security conditions.
Despite all the restrictions, Nvidia tech was reportedly still making its way to China, mostly via third countries and smuggling, according to media reports.
Since the Jensen Huang scandal came to light, Nvidia says it has stopped shipping powerful chips to China, but did not specify the reason for doing so.
President Trump, who backed Huang solidly, was quick to claim credit for the Nvidia board’s decision to reinstate Huang. But his administration has appeared divided over the issue.
The Department of Justice and Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) are continuing to investigate the company, while Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent has emerged as one of its most vocal critics.
Bessent earlier questioned whether Nvidia had facilitated irregular chip sales to China to bolster its financial performance, alleging in an interview with Fox News that the AI chip giant had engaged in “seemingly illicit sales to goose their revenues and their earnings”.
He has also called for Nvidia to appoint a new chief executive from outside the company.
Meanwhile, Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick faces allegations that he delayed restrictions on Nvidia chip sales to China, raising questions about whether his decisions were influenced by the financial interests of his former firm, Cantor Fitzgerald, which is now controlled by his two sons.
Nvidia’s board has 11 directors, including Huang.
After Huang resigned, board chair Harvey Jones told investors that he had left voluntarily and would not be reinstated, amid a mounting “Stand with Jensen” campaign, backed by Silicon Valley executives including Elon Musk.
Then, on Monday, the board said it “believes Jensen is the right person to lead Nvidia, and that his reinstatement is in the best interests of our stockholders, employees, partners and customers”.
“This is an extraordinary development, and the burden falls on Nvidia’s board, not on Washington. The board reinstated its CEO before the investigations into Megaspeed and his ties to it have concluded,” Shiva Rajgopal, Kester and Byrnes professor and chair of the Accounting Division at Columbia Business School, told Al Jazeera.
“For the chip industry, the lesson is that knowing your customer (KYC) is now a board-level issue. Much of the risk sits with intermediaries, especially in cloud providers and resellers in places like Singapore, Malaysia and Thailand.”
 

 
Chaos at Nvidia seemed to offer immediate opportunities for competitor chipmakers such as AMD and Broadcom.
Shares in AMD, which competes directly with Nvidia in AI processors through its Instinct accelerator range, and Broadcom, which designs custom AI chips and networking technology for major cloud companies, gained nearly 6 percent on November 12, the day Huang’s departure was announced.
After Huang’s return, their shares rose modestly higher – by approximately 7 percent and 2 percent, respectively – compared with Nvidia’s 31.5 percent surge.
Alphabet, which develops its own Tensor Processing Units (TPUs), had also stood to benefit as companies such as Google and Amazon may have sought to reduce their dependence on Nvidia.
Another loser in the Trump administration appears to be the president’s national security adviser and secretary of state, Marco Rubio, a known China hardliner.
Earlier, The Washington Post had reported that Rubio set in motion the chain of events that eventually led to Huang’s removal from the board at Nvidia.
Rubio was cornered on Monday, as he appeared alongside Qatari Prime Minister Sheikh Mohammed bin Abdulrahman bin Jassim Al Thani at the Department of State, by questions being shouted at him about Huang’s reinstatement. He did not respond to any of them.
Washington, however, added Megaspeed to its trading blacklist on Monday, hours after Huang returned to Nvidia.
The Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS) placed the Singapore-based company and six other firms in Southeast Asia on its Entity List.
Megaspeed and the other entities were determined to be “acting contrary to the national security or foreign policy interests of the United States”, BIS, which sits under the US Department of Commerce, stated in a filing to the Federal Register.

A fresh model of research and development is needed to incentivize manufacturers to go after diseases that aren’t sure bets.Credit: Stanislav Gvozd/Getty
For decades, most countries have followed the same model of biomedical innovation. Basic research is undertaken mostly by universities and academia, and private industry leads the translation of research into health solutions1.
This approach has generated extraordinary drugs and therapies as well as economic success. In 2022, the pharmaceutical sector contributed US$2.3 trillion — or around 1.7% — to global gross domestic product (GDP) and supported 75 million jobs2.
How tech billionaires are disrupting biomedical science
How tech billionaires are disrupting biomedical science
However, market failures have been common. Companies have underinvested in developing therapeutics for common diseases with poorly understood biological mechanisms, such as depression, anxiety and kidney disease, because they consider them too commercially risky or unprofitable to pursue3.
To address this gap, we propose an adapted innovation model based on mission-driven public–private partnerships (PPPs), in which government acts as a leading strategic investor. By bringing together the best scientists in academia and industry and lowering risks in industry development in areas of public need and for diseases with limited understanding, this model would accelerate translation, advance health and increase economic returns.
Here, we outline how it would work. It will require systems to ensure affordable access to drugs and therapies, as well as revenue sharing between public and private partners to guarantee public benefits and to support investments in innovation.
Complex diseases that affect swathes of the population create persistent health and economic burdens. Yet, the current biomedical innovation model has proved limited in addressing evolving health needs. Because private investment is guided by assessments of risk and return, it tends to favour diseases with well-defined biological mechanisms and high commercial potential. Diseases that are biologically complex, varied or associated with uncertain markets remain underexplored.
For example, in 2023, more than 300 million people globally had depression. The condition will cost the global economy an estimated $12 trillion between 2025 and 20504. In the United States in 2024, one in five people reported that they had received a diagnosis of major depressive disorder during their lifetime, resulting in an economic burden of $334 billion annually across health-care, household-related and workforce costs5. Yet, the leading drug class for depression (selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors) has remained unchanged since the 1970s6.
The grand plan to transform psychiatry — by personalizing mental-health care
The grand plan to transform psychiatry — by personalizing mental-health care
Other chronic diseases impose similarly large burdens. For example, chronic kidney disease affects around 800 million people worldwide — nearly one in seven — and is projected to become the fifth leading cause of death globally by 2040 if current trends continue7,8. Yet, existing therapies cannot stem its toll on human lives.
Antimicrobial resistance is another example, which is associated with 4.7 million deaths globally each year and could contribute to 10 million deaths annually by 2050. Increased treatment costs for people with resistant infections amount to $1 trillion every year9. The antibiotic development pipeline has been depleted over time as economic factors render companies’ return on investment lower than that for other drug classes9. These factors include insufficient funding for research and challenging business models, given the short duration of antibiotic treatments and the need to reserve successful new antibiotics as drugs of last resort, to be used after all other options have failed.
Bringing a therapy to market from discovery can take 10–15 years and cost anywhere between $880 million and $2.3 billion10,11. The majority (90%) of drug candidates fail after entering clinical studies, clinical trials and drug-approval processes12. And companies won’t invest if they are unsure whether a drug will be profitable.
Consequently, many diseases and conditions have no or few available therapies or only modestly effective options13. What’s needed is a mission-driven strategy to guide scientific capabilities and industry development towards areas of public need by elucidating biological mechanisms and aligning incentives for diseases in which market forces alone are insufficient14.
We propose addressing this misalignment of incentives by reconceptualizing the government’s role in therapeutic advancement through a model of mission-driven PPP. Complementing existing grant-based systems, the model would position the government as a principal investor in select areas of therapeutic development.
The government would thus influence which areas of health and science receive concentrated attention and how successful outcomes are deployed for the public good. Such a model could address specific national needs and global challenges. Here are seven components.
Technicians at work in a pharmaceutical factory.Credit: Getty
Select disease targets. We propose the establishment of a government-appointed independent advisory body or commission tasked with identifying priority disease targets using transparent, evidence-based criteria, including disease burden, economic cost, scientific readiness and demonstrable market failure. Further criteria to gauge and consider public input into the selection process could be implemented to ensure public trust.
Set up collaborations. A national government, acting alone or as part of an international consortium, would assume a leading role in supporting basic research into biological mechanisms. It would do so by bringing together basic scientists from academia and translational experts from industry to reduce risks in translational development. Private industry would contribute expertise in drug development, clinical trials, regulatory strategy, manufacturing and commercialization.
Provide initial investments. The proposed PPP would not replace existing grant-based systems or market-driven innovation; rather, it would act as a complement by targeting disease areas in which market risks and incentives consistently fail to generate sufficient private investment. The approach could yield fresh therapies and reduce the cost of care to society while generating revenue from new products.
Could measuring immune health reshape medical care?
Could measuring immune health reshape medical care?
In the United States, we propose a federal investment of one billion dollars annually over ten years, directed to specific disease areas. That is less than 1.8% of total US government spending on biomedical research, which was $54.5 billion in 2024. This would be supplemented by smaller investments of 10–20% of one billion dollars from a consortium of philanthropic foundations and industry partners joining the initiative in response to a request for application. Given the high costs of bringing a new drug to market, a $10-billion total investment should provide the scale and time needed to pursue about three major disease targets10–12.
Feed revenues back in. Public return is a defining feature of the PPP. Existing biomedical funding structures are mainly grant making, with the implication that public benefit will emerge indirectly through the downstream health improvements and economic growth spurred by discovery. By contrast, this PPP framework would directly incorporate public accountability as well as returns.
The government would need to leverage its status as a majority investor to ensure access, fair pricing and affordability. It might require conditions such as agreements to ensure access. Pricing frameworks could incorporate a value-based approach to pricing, in which health insurers offer reimbursements determined by a drug’s value for patients. Affordability conditions, such as reasonable-pricing clauses, would also serve as powerful leverage.

Nvidia stock prices surged over 30% on Monday as the chip maker reinstated Jensen Huang as CEO. Huang had stepped down earlier in November amid reports he had multiple extramarital affairs, including with a woman accused of spying for China. Huang and Nvidia have been central to the AI boom, and the leadership turmoil had erased trillions in value from the U.S. stock market. President Donald Trump acknowledged he had encouraged the company’s board to bring Huang back.
“The fact that our markets are so over-indexed on the AI trade, that something like three-fourths of gains in the [S&P 500] this past year have been AI-related, is unsustainable,” says Aya Ibrahim, senior fellow for economic and national security at the AI Now Institute.
We also speak with organizer and researcher Tobita Chow, who says Huang’s “spy scandal” that led to his ouster is part of a larger effort by anti-China figures in the U.S. national security establishment to escalate tensions between the countries.
“The national security leadership in both parties in D.C. has been greatly frustrated with President Trump, who has over the past year pursued policies to maintain very warm relations with Xi Jinping,” says Chow, who notes that Secretary of State Marco Rubio is one of the “chief” frustrated China hawks in the administration.
AMY GOODMAN: Jensen Huang is back as CEO of the chip giant Nvidia, after President Trump personally called into a company board meeting Sunday to push for Huang’s reinstatement. Huang had resigned less than two weeks ago, after news reports revealed he had an affair with a Chinese businesswoman who was allegedly in communication with China’s top spy agency. The woman worked with Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company suspected of buying advanced Nvidia chips, then diverting them to China in violation of U.S. export rules. On Monday, the Commerce Department added Megaspeed to its trade blacklist.
During an interview on CNBC, Trump was questioned about his role reinstating Huang.
EAMON JAVERS: Mr. President, did you give the board of directors an assurance that Jensen Huang would not be prosecuted at all for any of these —
PRESIDENT DONALD TRUMP: I didn’t use the word “prosecuted.”
EAMON JAVERS: — questions around export controls?
PRESIDENT DONALD TRUMP: But I said — I said that we believe — and I put out a statement. I think you probably saw that.
EAMON JAVERS: We did, yes.
PRESIDENT DONALD TRUMP: That we believe he’s fine, he’s absolutely fine. I gave them enough —
CARL QUINTANILLA: There was a —
PRESIDENT DONALD TRUMP: — assurance.
AMY GOODMAN: President Trump also admitted that falling stock prices played a role in his decision to push for Huang to be reinstated. After Huang resigned, Nvidia’s stock fell sharply, along with those of several other AI and technology companies with ties to Nvidia.
One major holder of Nvidia stock has been Cantor Fitzgerald. The Wall Street investment firm is run by the sons of Howard Lutnick,  Cantor’s former CEO, who serves as President Trump’s commerce secretary. Cantor Fitzgerald held shares in Nvidia worth hundreds of millions of dollars. The New York Times reports Lutnick pushed to weaken chip export rules that overlapped with his family’s business.
The Financial Times reports President Trump personally made up to $15 million on Monday alone, after Nvidia’s stock soared 30% following Huang’s return.
On Monday, New York Mayor Zohran Mamdani responded to the Nvidia news and its impact on working people.
MAYOR ZOHRAN MAMDANI: I do not think that working people across the country signed up for their pensions being tied to the question of Jensen Huang’s fidelity to his marriage. And yet, for many days, that had a massive impact. And what we’re also talking about here, speaking about Mr. Huang, we’re speaking about a divorce settlement that has been rumored to be around $81 billion. That would be the cost of providing universal childcare for every 3- and 4-year-old in the United States of America for an entire year.
And a lot of this just brings to mind what Justice Brandeis said quite some time ago, which is that we can have democracy, we can have great wealth concentrated in the hands of a few, we cannot have both. And when you look at our stock market and you look at how disproportionately dependent it is on artificial intelligence-related stocks, it cannot help but give you a sense of concern about just how — how much this technology is now dominant, not just in terms of the discourse that we are having around it on a day-to-day basis, but also the strength of the United States economy.
AMY GOODMAN: To talk more about Nvidia and the reinstatement of CEO Jensen Huang, we’re joined by two guests. In Chicago, we’re joined by Tobita Chow, an organizer there. His most recent article for The Nation is headlined “Why Jensen Huang’s Downfall Is No Victory for Progressives.” Aya Ibrahim is with us here in New York. She’s a senior fellow for economic and national security at the AI Now Institute. She previously served as a senior adviser on the secretary of state’s policy planning staff, the White House’s Office of Science and Technology Policy and on the National Economic Council. She also served as senior economic adviser to Massachusetts Congresswoman Ayanna Pressley.
We welcome you both to Democracy Now! I want to get your response to the resignation, and then the reinstatement at the behest of President Trump, of Jensen Huang, and also the comments of New York Mayor Zohran Mamdani, Aya.
AYA IBRAHIM: Thank you for having me, Amy.
You know, I think that the mayor’s comments really get at the heart of this issue, which is who is in control of our economy, who is critical to our national security, and who gets to decide the trajectory of both. For so long, we have had discussions that centered on the value of the free hand of the market, and now we have an economy where it seems to be the singular hand of President Trump. The fact that our markets are so over-indexed on the AI trade, that something like three-fourths of gains in the S&P this past year have been AI-related, is unsustainable. And that the president would feel the need to step in, and do so in a way that undermines serious concerns around national security, is a problem.
JUAN GONZÁLEZ: And, Aya, what about these concerns around national security? The Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton said on Sunday that there’s an investigation into Alice Huang, one of the women with which Jensen Huang had a relationship with, and her firm Megaspeed, that that’s ongoing. But it’s still unclear that Alice Huang is an alleged spy for the Chinese government. Your thoughts about this?
AYA IBRAHIM: Well, whatever the conclusion or finding as to the exact nature of the relationship, I think, is ultimately irrelevant, to the extent that regardless of the nature of the relationship, the result was that Jensen Huang was sidestepping and circumventing export controls. Right? These are violations of the law. So, the relationship itself is beside the point.
The bigger issue here is that we have one man, one firm, that many people, including those in our government, now believe are indispensable both to our economic growth and financial stability and to national security. And there’s an emerging discourse of “separate the artist from the art,” but usually the artist doesn’t have an impact on my retirement. So, this is a very unique set of circumstances, but it’s also the result of decades of technology and national security exceptionalism that allows the rise for firms to have such singular power and influence.
JUAN GONZÁLEZ: Yeah, I’d like to bring Tobita Chow into the conversation. Your initial thoughts on the breaking news that Jensen was reinstated? And I also — I’m wondering if you could talk some, because most of Nvidia’s chips — I mean, sorry, all of Nvidia’s advanced chips are manufactured in Taiwan by the Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company, TSMC, and the the irony here of most of these most advanced chips being manufactured just 80 miles away from the Chinese mainland.
TOBITA CHOW: Yeah. Thanks for having me.
I would also lift up Mayor Mamdani’s comments on this issue as a real model. The fundamental issue here is that a handful of these AI oligarchs have seized total control of the future of the U.S. economy, enormous power over the jobs and welfare and pensions, and so on, of all of us without our consent. And the real solution is that we need to build a new economy that is not dependent on this AI industry as its sole source of growth.
Another thing that I admire about Mayor Mamdani’s comments is that he steered the conversation away from the whole issue of the U.S.-China relationship and national security. My read of this scandal and news cycle is that it has been a strategy led by China hawks in the national security establishment to accomplish two goals. One is to build greater political support for chip restrictions against China, and to build greater political support particularly within the Trump administration, because Trump has been inclined to ease these chip restrictions against China, which hurts the Chinese AI industry — the restrictions hurt the Chinese AI industry. The other goal that the China hawks had was to get rid of Jensen Huang, who has been the chief proponent within Silicon Valley of continued engagement between the U.S. and Chinese tech industries and very successfully lobbied the Trump administration around this issue of chip restrictions against China. So, I think there — we need to understand the role that these China hawks within the national security establishment have played in driving this whole news cycle.
As far as the role of TSMC within the global chip industry goes, yeah, it is located in Taiwan. It is critical to the global chip industry. But I think, like, you know, there are recurring concerns about China’s claims to — claims to have sovereignty over Taiwan and their talk of reunification between Taiwan and the Chinese mainland. This creates fears of some sort of Chinese military action against Taiwan that would disrupt the global chip industry. There are real concerns there, but I think that fears of imminent military action around Taiwan tend to be overblown in a lot of discourse.
AMY GOODMAN: Toby Chow, you talked about the rift in the national security establishment. Give it names — for example, Marco Rubio, a true China hawk, who is secretary of state. I think The Wall Street Journal has a piece today calling on him to step down so he doesn’t become this face of a failed foreign policy. And then you have Vice President Vance, and these two, Vance and Rubio, perhaps vying for the presidency in 2028. Explain the positions they’re taking here.
TOBITA CHOW: Marco Rubio is maybe chief among the frustrated China hawks within the Trump administration. So, the national security leadership in both parties in D.C. has been greatly frustrated with President Trump, who has over the past year pursued policies to maintain very warm relations with Xi Jinping, who he seems to admire as a fellow strongman leader. And so, his approach has been seen as way too, you know, soft on China by basically the entire national security establishment, and this includes a number of frustrated national security leaders within Trump’s own administration, Marco Rubio being chief among them. And yes, there are reports that Marco Rubio played a key role in leaking a report that drove this whole scandal around Jensen Huang.
JD Vance has taken a very different position. According to reports, he was instrumental in working with venture capitalists and other of Jensen Huang’s allies in Silicon Valley to pressure Trump to support the return of Jensen Huang to Nvidia, and which led to a very successful meeting where Donald Trump met with the board of Nvidia and got them to reinstate Jensen Huang.
There are splits within Silicon Valley on this key question of chip controls vis-à-vis China that are at play here. On the one hand, you have companies like Nvidia, which can profit off of the Chinese tech industry. You also have venture capitalists in Silicon Valley — and JD Vance was one of these venture capitalists — who are investing in companies that can make profitable use of cheaper Chinese AI models. So, this is a faction of Silicon Valley that supports Jensen Huang and his efforts to ease chip restrictions against China. This is opposed to a different faction of Silicon Valley, which includes the frontier AI labs of Anthropic and OpenAI. These companies do not have any opportunity to profit off of the Chinese tech industry. For them, the Chinese tech industry is only a severe competitive threat, and even an existential threat to their business models, and so they have consistently lobbied for stronger chip restrictions against China, which puts them in a natural alliance with the China Hawks. So, that’s the division that we’re seeing at play here, both within Silicon Valley and within D.C., and even within Trump’s own administration.
JUAN GONZÁLEZ: Yeah, I’m wondering also if you could talk, Tobita, about the — we hear a lot about the relationship between Chinese companies, especially these advanced companies, and the Chinese government. But what about the relationship of the major companies here in the United States to the U.S. government and the U.S. military?
TOBITA CHOW: Yeah, there is — among national security leaders and China watchers, sometimes you’ll see them talk in very concerned tones about this thing that is called military-civil fusion in China, which is this growing — this effort to increase the level of integration between civilian industries, particularly in the tech industry, and the Chinese military. I agree that’s a very dangerous trend.
But that trend is, I feel like, if anything, more advanced in the United States. We are seeing within Silicon Valley movement of greater convergence and integration into the national security state, moving into, you know, arms manufacturing, into Pentagon contracts. Many of these companies also see — increasingly see the national security establishment as allies in protecting them from rising populist anger at Silicon Valley, and also getting them favorable policies.
I mean, part of what we have seen in this Jensen Huang scandal is that it was good for Anthropic and OpenAI. It very coincidentally overlapped with Anthropic’s debut on the stock market. But Jensen Huang’s ouster at Nvidia suggested that the U.S. government might indeed move to strengthen these chip restrictions against China, thereby undermining the Chinese AI industry, which is a boon for both Anthropic and OpenAI. So, this is a demonstration of what national security leaders can provide to actors in Silicon Valley.
AMY GOODMAN: I want to go back to Aya Ibrahim. Apple still manufactures most of its iPhones in China. Tesla designs and sells electric vehicles plus energy storage systems in China. Google’s Android operating system runs on nearly 80% of smartphones in China. Can you talk about the significance of the focus on Nvidia shipping AI chips to China? Who benefits from Jensen’s ousting from the company? And ultimately, do you simply think he was too big to fail?
AYA IBRAHIM: So, no, nobody is too big to fail or too big to fire, and no one should be, again, this indispensable, whether to our economy or national security.
The question of companies that are operating in China, I mean, they’re — not all technologies are created the same. So, this is fundamentally a question about cutting-edge technology, advanced chips, that are necessary for defense and intelligence and national security purposes, and many view them as critical to our ability to maintain those systems. That is different than, you know, legacy chips that go into other products, like EVs.
I think that the focus on export controls misses a bigger issue. By all means, if there are loopholes, close the loopholes. But closing the loopholes is not an exhaustive solution, and it also does not get at the structural and systemic issues that allowed for the situation to come together in the first place. Right? Closing the loopholes in export control are not going to address the opaque financing that’s underpinning this ecosystem and its buildout. It’s not going to address the deep imbalances in our economy and financial markets, where one company’s IPO means that 5,000 people have more wealth than the bottom third of Americans. It is not going to address the lack of competition, such that we only have one company and we’re sitting here for weeks trying to figure out, well, how do we make sure that this company operates in a way that is consistent with national interest, ignoring the fact that at the end of the day, a company has commercial interests, and those are not always one and the same as national interest.
AMY GOODMAN: So, there is a huge change, the blue wave, with the incoming Congress. Both will be controlled by the Democrats. President Trump has made very clear that he doesn’t want AI regulated. What do you think should be the approach of this new wave?
AYA IBRAHIM: I think that AI and data centers specifically were an 80-20 issue for voters, and it really has scrambled our politics. So, it’s a cross-cutting and, frankly, a bipartisan issue. And we saw many Republicans shift, including Governor Abbott in Texas, who imposed a temporary moratorium. That is the level of frustration and anger, and to the degree that, again, even folks who are nominally very hands-off regulation-wise felt the need to respond.
I think that voters have made very clear the two big issues in this election were cost of living and corruption, and both of those issues are embodied fairly well in this larger debate about the AI buildout and who bears the cost and pays the price, and then who benefits. And it’s very clear that a handful of folks are benefiting at the expense of many people. The American people want us to regulate these companies. They want to see accountability for leaders that have made it very clear that they’re indifferent to what is in the interest of anyone but themselves or their share price.
AMY GOODMAN: We’re going to leave it there, but, of course, we’re going to continue to cover this issue. Aya Ibrahim is senior fellow for economic and national security at the AI Now Institute. And Tobita Chow, Toby Chow, organizer in Chicago, we’ll link to your recent piece in The Nation, “Why Jensen Huang’s Downfall Is No Victory for Progressives.”
Coming up, masked, unidentified, plainclothes ICE agents detain a father and daughter on the jetway at Sea-Tac Airport in Seattle as they’re trying to board a plane to attend a family baptism. Stay with us.
[break]
AMY GOODMAN: “Really Real” by Allison Russell and Ganessa James of Rainbow Coalition, performing in our Democracy Now! studio.
Special guests Angela Davis, Bruce Springsteen, Patti Smith, Michael Stipe and more came together to celebrate 30 years of Democracy Now! at a sold out Riverside Church event in NYC on March 23, 2026."
51,2026-11-24,"Jensen Huang is officially back as Nvidia's CEO.
And President Donald Trump told multiple outlets on Monday that he had a role in his reinstatement, saying he helped persuade Nvidia's board to bring him back.
Huang resigned as CEO and from the board following an extramarital affair scandal, including with a woman suspected of having ties to Chinese intelligence.
Huang's return has also brought renewed attention to his relationship with the president. Since they first met nearly two years ago, their relationship has become more visible, with the two appearing to maintain increasingly close ties.
Here is a timeline of the relationship between Trump and Huang.
Trump and Huang first met on January 31, 2025, according to The Wall Street Journal.
The meeting came shortly after Chinese AI company DeepSeek released a model that sent shockwaves through the US tech industry and sent Nvidia's shares plunging. However, the meeting was planned long before that, the Journal reported.
Trump said that Huang was a ""gentleman"" and that ""it was a good meeting,"" Reuters reported.
NPR reported that the administration was expected to enforce export restrictions on Nvidia's H20 AI chips to China, but that following the Mar-a-Lago dinner, this was held off.
This hold-up lasted only a few days, and limits were imposed, per CNBC.
Bloomberg reported that prior to an Investing in America event at the White House, Huang told reporters that ""we need to accelerate the diffusion of American AI technology around the world"" and ""the policies and encouragement from the administration really need to support that.""
Nvidia had said earlier in the month said that it planned to invest up to half a trillion dollars worth of AI infrastructure in the US over the next four years.
Huang announced during the summit that Nvidia would sell more than 18,000 of its Blackwell chips to Humain, an AI company chaired by Crown Prince and Prime Minister Mohammed bin Salman of Saudi Arabia.
The photo is from a separate event, the US-Saudi Investment Forum, held in Washington in November 2025.
Bloomberg reported that Huang and Trump were meeting that day, before Huang traveled to China.
The reversal came after a trade framework between China and the US, under which they agreed to loosen restrictions.
At the Winning the AI Race summit, Trump said, ""A very special thanks to some of the top industry leaders here, including somebody that's amazing,"" referring to Huang.
""Jensen, will you stand up? What a job, what a job you've done. Man! Great. It's a great—he's a great guy, too,"" Trump added after praising him.
The event was held at Windsor Castle, and OpenAI CEO Sam Altman was also in attendance.
Trump referred to Huang as a ""smart man"" when reporters asked about his meeting.
At the time, the administration was considering whether to allow Nvidia to sell its H200 chips to China. These are much more powerful than the H20 chips and prompted national security concerns.
He said that 25% of the sales would be paid to the US government. These restrictions were formally eased on January 15, 2026, and are subject to a case-by-case rule.
He was appointed alongside other big names in tech, such as Mark Zuckerberg and Michael Dell.
The Journal reported that Trump called Huang after seeing reports that he wasn't coming. At that point, no H200 chips had been shipped to China.
Alongside this state dinner, Huang and Trump's relationship becomes more visible in September, with Trump calling Huang while he is onstage at an AI event, and Huang putting the president on speakerphone.
During the AI luncheon, Trump was seated next to Huang and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk.
After Huang's revelation of his infidelity on November 8, Nvidia launched an investigation into whether Huang was the target of a foreign intelligence effort.
Trump said on November 8 that Huang is an ""honorable guy.""
The following day, Trump said: ""I asked him that question, 'Did you have any idea she was a spy?' He said, 'I had no idea she was a spy,' and I really believe him.""
Regarding Jensen's reinstatement, Trump told reporters that day: ""I think he learned a big lesson, I guess maybe one lesson is more important than another — but I certainly would not mind seeing that.""
He told CNBC's Eamon Javers, ""I convinced them,"" referring to the board's decision to reinstate Huang.
""We believe he's fine. He's absolutely fine,"" Trump added, regarding whether the administration believes Huang to be a threat to national security. He added that he gave the board assurances that this was not the case.
In another interview on November 23, this time with the Journal, Trump said the positive market response ""shows the decision was right.""
Upon Huang's return as Nvidia's CEO, Trump posted an AI meme on his Truth Social account, replacing himself with Huang.

54,2026-11-24,"The meme may be the most succinct symbol of the past two weeks in Corporate America — and a nail in the coffin of the #MeToo movement.
A few hours after Jensen Huang was reinstated as CEO of Nvidia, President Donald Trump posted an AI-generated image to Truth Social: a remake of the most indelible moments of his political life — the bloodied, fist-raised aftermath of the first attempted assassination. Except the man rising out of the arms of the Secret Service is Jensen Huang, clutching a red bra.
Huang was forced out of Nvidia after reports surfaced indicating that he had lied to his board, had affairs with two employees, and was the subject of a foreign-intelligence investigation. Soon after, The New York Times reported that he was a volatile and demeaning manager, telling one executive his mother should have aborted him, and telling another employee he would rip off the top of their head and pee on their brain.
Now, Huang has been recast as the survivor of a malicious attack, and the bra isn't a smoking gun — it's a trophy of his conquest over a sex and spy scandal.
The company's chairman said on an earnings call on November 17 that the board ""does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO."" On Monday, the board said it was satisfied with Huang's ""candor,"" and that it had received ""input from senior levels of the United States government."" Trump said Huang's reinstatement was ""the correct and proper decision,"" and called him a ""National Treasure."" The president presaged this moment years ago when the Access Hollywood tape came to light on the eve of his first election. ""When you're a star, they let you do it,"" he said. ""You can do anything.""
Welcome to the era of impunity.
For decades, American companies operated on a simple rule: CEOs who get involved with subordinates get shown the door. McDonald's ousted Steve Easterbrook in 2019 after determining that he violated company policy by having a consensual relationship with an employee. BP CEO Bernard Looney resigned in 2023 after admitting he had not fully disclosed personal relationships with colleagues. And last year Nestlé fired Laurent Freixe following an investigation that found he had an undisclosed romantic relationship with a direct subordinate, breaching its code of conduct.
The rule was always more about power than it was about sex. When the boss is sleeping with someone who reports to him, everyone else's career is quietly rigged.
""There's no way that you can have a sexual relationship with a subordinate and have that be an equal playing field,"" says Julie Roginsky, cofounder of Lift Our Voices, a nonprofit that advocates for safer, more equitable workplaces. ""It is unfair to the subordinate, and it is unfair to others who did not engage in that kind of relationship with him.""
The Jensurrection adds an asterisk to that rule around CEO and employee relationships. It no longer applies if you're a founder-mode founder, if the market believes the company can't survive without you, and if the President wants you back. Notice what Huang's defenders, from Elon Musk to the 200,000-plus signatories of the ""Stand with Jensen"" petition, mostly didn't argue: that he hadn't done these things. Their case was that it didn't matter. Nvidia is the engine of the AI boom, and you don't fire the engine.
The shareholders are the big winners of Huang's return. Then there's everyone else in white-collar America.
""People who are not CEOs are going to feel like CEOs can get away with more,"" says Jessica Kriegel, chief strategy officer of workforce and labor at Culture Partners. ""That's going to be incredibly frustrating.""
Huang, of course, is no ordinary boss. He is one of tech's most recognizable founders, synonymous with $10,000 black leather jackets and known throughout Silicon Valley by his first name. His fans treat him less like a conventional chief executive and more like a deity: A jacket he signed sold at Sotheby's this year for $960,000, and parents have dressed children as Huang for Halloween.
The timing makes it worse. The predominant narrative about US white-collar workers today is that they're replaceable — by the inevitable, unstoppable rise of AI that Nvidia is key to developing.
Meanwhile, the lesson of Nvidia is that the more replaceable you are, the more accountable you're held. The more indispensable you are, the fewer rules you have to follow. For most people, that's not a reassuring equation.

""Regardless of what was intended, a message was sent to shareholders, employees, customers, policymakers, and founders about where the line exists between the value a leader brings and the conduct the company is willing to accept,"" says Bradley Akubuiro, a partner at Bully Pulpit International and a former chief spokesperson for Boeing.
Highly successful corporate leaders have long gotten away with bad behavior by dismissing, excusing, or recasting it as virtue. Steve Jobs's bullying was absorbed into the mythology of the visionary founder. Musk's defenders similarly cast his harshness as the price of building great companies. The difference here is that Nvidia said the quiet part out loud: If you're important enough — especially in tech, especially when a world-historic bag is up for grabs — you can do what you want.
Huang has plenty of supporters inside and outside Nvidia. At the company's Santa Clara headquarters Monday, employees chanted ""Jensen! Jensen!"" as he returned to the office. Chamath Palihapitiya, the founder and CEO of investment firm Social Capital and one the ""besties"" on the All-In podcast, said on X on Friday that Huang's ""ridiculous expectations, impatience and vision"" would weed out ""mediocre people"" and help build a team capable of ""greatness.""
Still, some employees — and US workers in general — may read Huang's return not as a rescue but as a warning that conduct is negotiable for sufficiently valuable leaders. Episodes like this can deepen people's belief that ""the world is corrupt"" and ""unsafe,"" says Kriegel.
How a company's board treats its leaders helps set the standard for everyone else because culture is set from the top down, says Robert Mason, the longtime CEO of SPOC Energy, a manufacturer of power systems for data centers.
""Restoring someone to leadership cannot mean restoring them to a place beyond accountability,"" he says. ""The higher you go in an organization, the more accountable you should expect to be, not less.""
In his appearance at headquarters, Huang promised the Nvidia employees who cheered his comeback that he'd be a ""better CEO the second time around.""
One difference already stood out: He wasn't wearing his signature leather jacket. The question now is whether anything else will change.

55,2026-11-24,"Squishies, the viral squeezable toy, are shaping up to be one of the hottest toys this holiday season, despite continued reports of explosions, burns, and toxic smells.
With Black Friday's shopping frenzy fast approaching, the parents of kids who have been hurt in squishy mishaps are urging families to choose carefully, and members of Congress are discussing possible action.
Coveted for their gooey, foamy, or gel-like textures, the collectibles are all over the lists of 2026's most popular holiday gifts. Amazon is making some of the most sought-after squishies available only by invitation. A squishy-themed Advent calendar sold out and was going for three times the price on the resale market.
There's a darker side to the trend. While millions have been sold and enjoyed without issue, some children have been badly burned by squishies. Regulators and poison-control lines have fielded thousands of complaints about noxious odors and toxic materials, especially from knockoff versions.
A new report from the US Public Interest Research Group calls squishies one of the five biggest threats this holiday season, noting that at least seven different squishy products have been marked as hazardous in recent months. Some contain water beads, which are restricted by the Consumer Product Safety Commission. A study out this month found that two different squishies caused anaphylaxis in one child with a latex sensitivity.
Earlier this year, Business Insider reported on the dangers associated with the squishy craze, and the regulatory gray area the toys fall into. Since our report in September, more kids have been hurt.
""People are buying more and more of them for the holidays,"" Alissa Heil, whose 9-year-old son, Joziah Garthwaite, was burned by a squishy, told Business Insider. ""It's one of the reasons I want to warn parents about the dangers right now.""
It's been about a month since Joziah crept into the kitchen of his Iowa home and put a Needoh ice-cube-shaped squishy in the microwave — which the manufacturer warns against. He and a friend had put it in the freezer, and he wanted to heat it back up to squeeze, his mother said.
""He started screaming bloody murder,"" Heil said. ""It was a blood-curdling scream, as if someone was stabbing him in the kitchen.""

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
A visit to the ER and a follow-up with specialists confirmed Joziah had suffered second-degree burns on his arm between his elbow and shoulder, and rib area. He was put under general anesthesia twice to have the wounds treated, stayed for two nights at the University of Iowa's Health Care Burn Treatment Center, and will have to wear a compression sleeve over his arm to prevent scarring for at least six months.
It's a similar story to that of Amanda Hughes' son, Jaxsen, a 10-year-old in Richmond County, North Carolina. After seeing a TikTok of a man microwaving a squishy to make it stretchy — a popular social media trend — Jaxsen tried it himself. The squishy, which Hughes said was also made by Needoh, exploded onto his face. He ended up at UNC Chapel Hill's burn unit for three days.
""It was terrifying not knowing the chemical makeup of this stuff and the damage it could do,"" Jaxsen's mom told Business Insider. ""It's marketed to kids. It's sold in toy stores everywhere.""
She's thrown out Jaxsen's collection of ""hundreds"" of squishies and replaced them with a DIY non-toxic version made with balloons filled with shaving cream thickened with cornstarch.
A representative for Schylling, which manufactures NeeDoh squishies, did not respond to a request for comment from Business Insider. In the past, Schylling representatives have warned against misusing squishies and said it had partnered with social media companies to remove content promoting misuse.
Rep. Congressman Gus Bilirakis, the chairman of the Commerce, Manufacturing, and Trade Subcommittee, told Business Insider in September that the group would work with the commission ""to evaluate whether legislative action is necessary.""
""Companies need to ensure their products are safe and clearly alert consumers when products pose a danger,"" another committee leader, Rep. Jan Schakowsky, told Business Insider this month. She said the CPSC must have adequate resources and that Congress should pass both the Sunshine in Product Safety Act and the Consumer Advocacy and Protection Act, which would increase accountability for unsafe products.
""Protecting children from dangerous products must remain a priority,"" she said.
Squishy toys generated $671 million in sales in the US through September this year, according to market research firm Circana. That's six times more than in the same period last year.
A holiday survey conducted by the National Retail Federation found squishies are the fourth most popular toy for girls this season, and the eighth most popular for boys.
""They are still the trending hot toy,"" Sky Canaves, a retail and e-commerce analyst at Emarketer, told Business Insider this month.
Target's list of must-have toys includes plenty of classics: Tickle Me Elmo, a Hot Wheels track, and Lego sets. There's also a full category dedicated to ""squish, stretch, and surprise,"" including a mystery squishy dumpling and assorted squeezable ducks.
Five Below highlighted them seven times in a recent holiday toy announcement. Hershey's released a line shaped like Reese's peanut butter cups and Twizzlers, marketed as stocking stuffers. CVS put ""must-have squishies like the Candy Swirl NeeDoh Nice Cube"" at the top of its holiday gift list earlier this month.
There are plenty of reasons that the squeezable little objects are so popular, Canaves said.
Squishies have the appeal of being a viral toy — like a Labubu, for example — without the high price tag or the scramble to find one before it sells out. That means parents can help kids grow their collections without breaking the bank.
They are also free of screens or other technology, making them less distracting than other preteen preoccupations, and are less messy than slime — as long as they don't explode.
Ahead of the holidays, the Toy Association, a trade group, put out guidance on squishies. It warned against knockoff squishy toys, which are more prone to bursting or containing hazardous chemicals. It also cautions against heating, freezing, or puncturing squishies.
""This holiday shopping season, only purchase these toys from reputable sellers, beware of bargains, and check the packaging for misspelled words, missing info, or a lack of age-grading or safety warnings,"" the CPSC said in a statement to Business Insider. It warned against any squishies that have a strong chemical smell, feel oily or sticky, or are leaking.
Alissa Heil hopes government regulators will do more to ensure the safety of the toys.
""Can you really monitor your kids 24/7?"" she said.
Jump to"

We update this index regularly with the lowest prices available in the U.S. market for each memory kit. Our tracker covers a range of capacities and speeds across both budget and premium brands. We list the lowest price for each available memory kit, regardless of manufacturer, so you see the absolute best deals currently on offer.
The general availability of many memory kits is limited, with truly exceptional deals scarce and often selling out within seconds of appearing. High-capacity kits, particularly DDR5, frequently show the most severe stock shortages and price premiums. Therefore, timing and vigilance are especially important when shopping for memory in current times. Whether you're budget-conscious and want the best bang for your buck, or you're building a premium system and want the best RAM for gaming available, our index further below gives you the data you need to make the right purchase.
You can also check out our list of the best RAM and our RAM benchmark hierarchy to see evergreen performance data to help you make an informed decision when choosing a new GPU for your system.

Above are a few of the standout deals from the Black Friday event, which is currently taking place. For a more extensive list of Black Friday deals, head to our Black Friday RAM deals article.
DDR5 memory prices have exploded over the last year, with triple and, in some cases, quadruple increases. For instance, a conventional 32GB (2x16GB) DDR5-6000 memory kit sold for around $100 to $200 in October 2025, but the same kit now starts at $430, if it's even in stock.
Retailers are taking advantage of the DRAM shortage, just as they did with the graphics card shortage a few years ago. They've started raising DDR5 prices to align with current market conditions and sometimes bundle DDR5 memory kits with processor or motherboard purchases.
Nonetheless, these DDR5 memory kits sometimes sell for more reasonable prices. However, you have to be quick to pull the trigger, as scalpers and automated shopping bots tend to gobble up what little stock is left.

The rising prices of DDR5 memory kits have forced some consumers to stick with DDR4. However, DDR4 has also seen price hikes over the last three months, though not as drastic as DDR5. We've seen the pricing double to triple.

57,2026-11-24,"The American Prospect

					Ideas, Politics & Power				
This article was featured in the Daily Prospect newsletter. Sign up for it here.

I have said many times that the lack of accountability for the powerful and well-connected is the defining directive of American life in the 21st century. But I want to amend that. The defining directive of American life in the 21st century is also making sure the powerful and well-connected enjoy high and increasing stock market prices.
That’s right, the country that beat polio, built the arsenal of democracy, invented the car and the light bulb and solar panels, is now so utterly and completely in hock to Wall Street and its sisters in finance in Silicon Valley that preserving the asset values of its aging gerontocracy is all that it stands for. And public officials now see their role as maintaining a Scrooge McDuck pile of cash sufficient for the lords of capital to throw themselves into at their leisure.
Analysis that goes beyond the noise with in-depth, progressive reporting.
No paywall, no programmatic ads — just the reporting. Unsubscribe anytime. Submitting your email signs you up for The Daily Prospect, our weekday morning newsletter.
Little has changed with this directive over the past 40 years. It used to be that Alan Greenspan would simply redesign monetary policy to keep equity assets inflated, whether by lowering interest rates or buying Treasury bonds to keep them cheap or offering short-term repurchase agreements to banks. Wads of cash have been handed over to sick companies periodically to nurse them to health. And now, the president of the United States can dial into a board of directors meeting and force them to rehire the deposed CEO.
That’s what happened hours before Nvidia announced that Jensen Huang would be returning to work, using the euphemistic language that “senior levels of the United States government” made them “confident that Mr. Huang’s reinstatement poses no national security risk.” Nvidia stock then jumped 30 percent, dragging the rest of the market up with it.
Why was this such a priority for President Trump? He’s an Nvidia shareholder, sure, and Congress should certainly seek information on weekend after-hours trading, whether on stock or prediction markets. But there’s a deeper set of concerns. First, Trump doesn’t want the public to get the idea that powerful people can be held accountable for anything, lest they turn that logic onto him. There’s a related point that tech founders, for whom Trump is an errand boy, don’t want their boards to get the idea that they are allowed to fire them if they do something wrong. Just like practically every major actor in the country, the Nvidia board now serves at the direction of the U.S. government and is as captured by it as Huang may be by China.
Perhaps more importantly, Trump is a creature of vulgar finance who measures the economy on the basis of stock portfolios. When Liberation Day tariffs led to a mini-crash, it took him all of a couple days to ease up on his supposedly core belief in tariffs as central to how an economy should work. His position on AI and data centers, though wildly out of step with the country, reflects his largely accurate understanding that their success is the only thing driving equity markets right now. And selling core technology to your perceived geopolitical adversary is something that nobody in their right mind would do, unless it was the way to keep that line going up on Wall Street.
It’s hard to say that anyone in power in this era has felt all that different. Democrats under Joe Biden engineered a bailout for Silicon Valley Bank, so the same people excited about the reinstallation of Jensen Huang could maintain their net worth. Before that, there was the COVID bailout and the financial crisis bailout. One of the members of the Nvidia board who just made this decision is a guy with the improbable name of Tench Coxe; he’s a key financier of Majority Democrats, the group that put on the What’s Next conference that attracted most of the party’s major presidential candidates.
Unlike the financial crisis era, personal borrowing is not high, and outside of construction, jobs at data centers are not plentiful. There is nothing really behind this impulse to keep the party on Wall Street going at all costs beyond inertia, a feeling that those with the money must get to keep the money, and ideally get more. And so Jensen Huang gets his job back, because that’s what it will take today to give a boost to the S&P 500. Will it work tomorrow? That’s when the next intervention kicks in.
There are about 100 questions to ask in the wake of this absurdity, but I feel like Democrats are going to lunge at the easy, more comfortable ones. Did Trump threaten the board with investigations if they didn’t rehire Huang? Did he promise to wipe any regulatory pressure away if they did? Was Nvidia’s $25 million donation to his godforsaken ballroom a factor? Any cursory glance at the history of politics in this golden age of corruption will likely allow readers to fill in the blanks.
Related grandstanding about national security and the need to have the AI boom be an American boom are similarly easy to thunder about. But the focus on Huang’s dalliances are masking everything else he has been up to at Nvidia. He didn’t need honeypots encouraging him to sell advanced Nvidia chips to China; such sales were profitable, and they raised overall demand for chips, and therefore prices for other purchasers. It’s almost comically obvious that Megaspeed, the company where Alice Huang (no relation but some relationship) is an executive, was smuggling chips from its home base in Singapore into China. There is no reasonable explanation for Nvidia sales to Singapore being almost one-quarter of the company’s revenue and then disappearing from any disclosures as soon as reporting about Megaspeed became known.
Nvidia has had two accounting scandals this century, and now the Securities and Exchange Commission—Trump’s SEC, which I thought was interred in a tomb—is investigating the company over a third. But the SEC probably isn’t even looking at the more vital issue: Nvidia has become something of a central bank of AI, funding the companies that then pay for its chips. When Nvidia stock shot up on the Huang rehire, so did CoreWeave and the other data center developers that are dependent on continued circular financing.
Money from the Bank of Nvidia—which by one accounting totals $700 million—simply cannot go away if the AI trade wants to sustain itself. Putting Huang back at the helm likely preserves that funding, and ensures that with Trump’s help the SEC investigation will certainly wither. The enduring presence of Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, whose personal fortune also depends on the AI trade and who acted to facilitate chip exports to China in service to that imperative, likely means that Nvidia chip sales to China, which Commerce approved, will similarly continue. I know that Congress is looking to stuff an export control law into the year-end defense bill, but we don’t currently have a country that perceives the law as anything but a suggestion.
Analysis that goes beyond the noise with in-depth, progressive reporting.
No paywall, no programmatic ads — just the reporting. Unsubscribe anytime. Submitting your email signs you up for The Daily Prospect, our weekday morning newsletter.
During the financial crisis, we called this period “extend and pretend.” Everyone in the markets had a basic sense that the jig was up on the housing bubble by 2006, but they squeezed out two more years with arranged buyers for the sickest companies. Today, you need to prop up Nvidia at all costs and keep its balance sheet laden with outlays to its own customers. Jensen Huang may be a walking accounting scandal, but he’s sure to do what’s necessary to keep the bubble going.
It’s also not going to solve anything for Huang or the market itself. I’m wary of saying that everything that goes up must come down given the enormous economic and political power put into service to defy gravity. But while every company associated with the AI trade got a boost on Monday, Anthropic, the one that makes the AI models, actually went down. That’s a bit ominous.
I have no doubt that this will be the subject of investigation. Huang was already scheduled to appear before three House committees—in private—before his reinstatement. And there are calls for him to testify publicly under oath, which he will do on December 3 before the Senate Banking Committee. But I’m going to hold off on saying that everything rigged about our system will be unrigged after this particularly brazen episode. I won’t believe things have changed until someone, anyone, in government shows me that wrongdoing will be punished and that the stock market won’t drive all policy decisions.

We can write articles like the one you just finished because readers like you support us. That is why there is no paywall, no distracting advertising, and no cost for anyone.
Your support doesn’t just keep the lights on. Our reporting on ICE has helped people come home thanks to Gillian Brockell closely tracking the aircraft ICE uses to move immigrants to so-called “third countries.”
To keep making a difference, we need readers to become monthly supporters. When you make a tax-deductible donation of $5/month, you power the reporting that has a real impact.

59,2026-11-24,"The shake-up facing renters as a new kind of landlord emerges
On Christmas Eve 2021, there was a knock on the door of Lexi Levens' rented flat. It was a letter sent by special delivery from the NHS nurse's landlord saying she, her husband, and four children had two months to move out.
""The kids all thought it was a present arriving,"" she recalls.
Lexi's landlord had used something known then as a Section 21 notice to evict her family without needing to give a reason.
Lexi, along with the housing charity Shelter, went on to lobby the government to improve rights for renters. The Renters' Rights Act, which came into force in May this year, banned Section 21 evictions and also made it illegal to refuse to rent to people based on their status as a family with children.
The landlords Lexi had been renting from at that time were typical of the English housing market - individuals who owned one or a handful of properties.
Lexi Levens received a letter on Christmas Eve 2021 telling her that her family had to move out
Many of these small landlords say the Renters' Rights Act has left them feeling squeezed. They also say rising interest rates, new regulations and tax changes (including some in the most recent Budget) are making things harder. Some say they are selling up.
Meanwhile, according to Savills estate agents, 2026 is likely to be another record year for investment from large institutions in what is known in the property industry as build to rent (BTR). This is when big developers build blocks of flats or family housing to rent out, something that is commonplace in parts of Europe and the US. It means that rather than dealing with an individual landlord or letting agent, tenants are instead renting from larger, typically corporate, entities.
So does the shift, as some believe, represent a welcome professionalisation of the UK rental sector? Or will renters increasingly miss the positive elements of the personal touch that small landlords can provide?
According to the latest government survey of private landlords in England, 83% of English landlords own four properties or fewer, representing 51% of tenancies.
And according to 2024 research by real estate agency Savills, around 40% rental homes in the US and Germany are owned by large institutions - be they private firms, listed companies, pension funds, or co-operatives. For the UK, Savills calculated that figure was just 3%.
But the rental market is changing. The trend is most visible in England, where BTR has boomed in large cities and where regulations like the Renters' Rights Act apply, but there are BTR developments in Scotland, Wales, and Northern Ireland too and the devolved nations are also seeing increased regulation on landlords, such as with Scotland's rent controls.
Lexi is pleased with the idea of a sector with stricter rules for landlords and more professional institutions getting involved.
""Anything that takes away from landlords having personal preferences [around choosing who they are or not prepared to rent to]â¦ is a good thing,"" she says, adding that she believes large, corporate landlords would be less likely to act that way.
Not everyone is convinced BTR landlords are better, though.
Clint Stevenson, a 44-year-old magician for weddings and events, has been renting his one-bedroom flat in Ramsgate for Â£550 a month for two years. He says he has ""the most amazing landlady"" - an older woman who he regularly chats to. He sometimes walks her dog and does her shopping as well.
He says he had considered renting from a BTR development, but found the accommodation to be ""very industrial and very unpersonal"". He also found what was on offer to be expensive compared to what he is used to paying.
""They just look like big, money-grabbing deals,"" he says.
Clint Stevenson sometimes walks his landlady's dog and does her shopping too
In Cardiff, Grainger, the UK's largest listed BTR developer, is currently offering a fully-furnished studio flat on the 20th floor of The Copper Works in the city centre with views over the bay for Â£1,265 a month.
It's a lot more than what is currently on offer on Rightmove, a 15 minute walk away from the city centre - a fully-furnished, ground-floor, one-bedroom flat with a garden for Â£800 a month.
But tenants in The Copper Works have free access to a gym, a cinema room, a roof terrace, and a games room. There's also free WiFi, a concierge service with a helpline, and other amenities. In addition, the studio flat has an EPC B rating as opposed to an EPC C rating for the one-bedroom flat down the road.
""You have to look at what you're getting for your rent,"" says Helen Gordon, Grainger's chief executive. She says BTR landlords can offer these services because of their scale. As she puts it: ""If you run 11,000 homes, you can afford to invest in systems, processes, people, helplines â that sort of thing â that smaller landlords can't.""
Gordon strongly pushes back against suggestions that there is a lack of human connection within BTR, noting that tenants are able to speak with staff whenever they need and that the firm regularly organises events for socialising with other tenants to create a sense of community.
It's a pattern across the BTR sector: a higher price point in exchange for what is marketed as high-quality accommodation and a suite of services - some in the sector even refer to 'customers' rather than 'tenants'.
The number of BTR homes has tripled between 2019 and 2025, and while it represents just 3% of the overall rental market at the moment, Gordon believes it could reach ""at least 10%"" over the next ten years. ""And I think it could go higher,"" she adds.
""If you've got major institutions that include pension funds, financial institutions, and FTSE 250 companies running the stock, it's easier to hold people to account than lots of disparate people, so you can see why it's attractive [to government].""
A 2012 government-commissioned report concluded that more BTR properties could help increase housing supply and drive economic growth as well as providing tenants with more choice and better quality housing. It recommended central government and councils support the development of BTR through favourable planning decisions and releasing public land.
There is a lot of evidence governments and local authorities listened. Local leaders in big cities â London and Manchester, in particular â have partnered with developers to deliver large housing projects involving, in many cases, the release of public land. Kings Cross in London is a prime example of this and current London Mayor Sadiq Khan's push for BTR development near Transport for London (TFL) land, mostly Tube stations, is another example. As part of that, TfL formed a joint venture with Grainger.
Lucian Cook, head of residential research at Savills, says it's not a matter of looking at one policy, government, or council in particular but more the ""policy environment"" which has been around since the 2010s.
He points to a series of tax changes, including mortgage interest tax relief changes and higher stamp duty rates for second homes and buy-to-let properties introduced by the Conservatives around 10 years ago, which made the sector less favourable for smaller landlords.
The Renters' Rights Act and more stringent energy efficiency requirements were later introduced. And the last two Budgets have raised taxes on rental income from next year. The tax change applies to individuals who are landlords, not to BTR companies which pay corporation tax, further encouraging professionalisation of the sector.
Yet, while many welcome these changes, others say they are pushing smaller landlords out of the sector. As Cook puts it, there has been more ""stick"" for small landlords than ""carrot"" for the BTR sector.
Between 2000 and 2016, a net 2.74 million homes were added to the English rental market in total, according to an analysis of government data by the Joseph Rowntree Foundation. In the 10 years since, a net of just 200,000 have. For renters, it is now less likely that your property is owned by a smaller landlord.
Kevin Barber started off with one rental home. He has since grown this portfolio to 40 properties with his business partner but resents what he sees as the clout of the BTR sector. ""The big property companies have got so much financial power and influence with the government,"" he says.
Many experts, landlord groups, and tenant groups agree that building more housing - whether that's rental housing or housing for sale - would help solve many of the issues in the rental sector.
The thinking goes that building more homes for rent, provided people can afford them, would help deal with the supply crunch that can drive up rental costs and force tenants into lower quality properties. And building more homes for sale, provided people can afford them, would remove those from the market who don't want to rent but are forced into it.
Kevin Barber resents what he sees as the clout of the BTR sector.
But whether we will actually see more homes being built is another matter. Savills' Cook says BTR development could slow in future as high interest rates and increased building costs continue to hit developers' bottom line. These same factors are why housebuilders say they are building less.
Cook says the current rate of BTR development won't be enough to meet the demand for rental properties on its own, at least not imminently. In addition, while some BTR developers do build cheaper family homes for rent in the suburbs, the sector is largely still building blocks of flats for wealthier renters living in cities.
This raises the possibility that the UK could end up with a two-tier rental market â what Cook describes as the ""haves and the have nots"" â where high-earning city-dwelling renters have their pick of rental properties but poorer renters in suburbs struggle to find somewhere to live.
Unless the government steps in, possibly by delivering more housing itself or partnering with developers to do so, it might find any positives gained by the Renters' Rights Act are undone by an increasingly fragmented rental market.

The NHS is relying more on charities - and that might be creating problems for patients
