61,2026-11-24,"Following closely on the heels of the launch of Volkswagen's first-ever electric camper van, the ID. Buzz California Cruise, the all-new ID. Buzz Beach grabs the baton and brings VW-factory all-electric van life to the United Kingdom. Beyond its market-specific name, the Beach carbon-copies the Cruise's playbook to the very last T, offering a convertible bed, dining set and full window kit in the Buzz of your choice. Is it worth the spend in a market with so many other cool tiny camper vans?  
Introduced for the UK market this month, the new ID. Buzz Beach brings the Beach name back to its rightful place. Just like conventional ICE-driven California Beach models, this one is a light camper/sleeper van without a full kitchen. That may be a dealbreaker for some, but by leaving the kitchen out, the Beach series is able to offer a wider bed that stretches from sidewall to sidewall without butting up against a kitchen block. 
In the case of the Buzz Beach, that means a 79 x 47-in (200 x 120-cm) foldable double bed. So far, Volkswagen has not said a word about a Buzz camper van with a slide-out or inbuilt kitchen like it offers on its ICE California models. 
If the Beach's layout is giving you a hard case of déjà vu, that's because it's the same one Volkswagen presented at the 2026 Caravan Salon in the ID. California Cruise aimed at continental Europe, as the Good Night Package developed as an aftermarket kit, and as the Buzz Tourer in the United States. The vans underpinning the campers aren't all exactly the same – the US-bound Tourer, for instance, is actually the Tourer 4Motion because it only comes as an all-wheel drive – but they all bring the same basic camping layout with a bed that stretches out atop the folded rear seats, black-out window blinds, ventilation inserts, and an outdoor dining set. 
Like the California Cruise, the ID. Buzz Beach can be ordered as a five-, six- or seven-seater.  When specced in six- or seven-seat form, the bed stores vertically in the load area behind the third row, whereas in the five-seater it stores horizontally in the load area, leaving cargo space underneath. 
All California Beach models also come with blackout curtains and ventilation grilles for the front windows. This ensures a comfortably dark, well-ventilated sleeping space. 
The outdoor dining set includes a folding table and two folding chairs. Much like the bed, it stores differently in the five-seater, disappearing away under the false floor. In six- and seven-seat Beach vans, the set stands up vertically in back next to the bed. 
As is the case with the new plug-in hybrid California camper van, the highlight of the new Buzz Beach is its overnight climate control capability. In fact, since it has a much larger battery pack, the ID. Buzz Beach can run its climate control off-grid for far longer than the California's eight hours. 
Using the ""Overnight"" mode in the touchscreen infotainment system, the Beach can run the climate control off of battery power for up to 48 hours. You'll want to be careful, of course, since you'll need some battery to get home or to a nearby charging station, but the capability to stay cool during summer heat spells and warm through plunging nighttime temperatures is ready to go without the separate camp furnace and air conditioning units you'd need on traditional ICE RVs. 
Beyond operating off-grid climate control, the ID. Buzz traction battery can also be used as an off-grid power station through the standard 230-V electrical socket on the exterior. Campers can power equipment and accessories like lighting, blenders and coffeemakers. 
Volkswagen hasn't released additional specs, but the UK-market ID. Buzz is available with five seats on a standard wheelbase van measuring 185 in (471 cm) in total length and as a seven-seater on the long-wheelbase platform measuring 195 in (496 cm) bumper to bumper. The six-seater is available on both wheelbases. Standard-wheelbase models come with a 79-kWh battery pack, while long-wheelbase variants have a slight upgrade to 86 kWh. Maximum range lists in at 300 miles combined (WLTP) on VW Commercial Vehicles' UK website. 
The all-new ID. Buzz Beach will start at £65,837 (approx. US$86,325) after factoring in VAT and on-the-road costs. Order books will open in the coming days, and options will include 20-in Stockton wheels and the cherry red/candy white two-tone paint scheme shown in photos. 
Volkswagen has yet to announce pricing for the US-market Buzz Tourer 4Motion light camper van. It originally planned to launch it as part of an updated MY2027 lineup after not selling an MY2026 ID. Buzz at all, but the latest indications are that the updated Buzz lineup will not return to the North American market until MY2028. Automotive News reported the delay in late September.
What's another year after more than two decades of no new VW camper vans? 
If and when it ever does arrive, the ID. Buzz Tourer 4Motion is slated to have the same camping setup and equipment as the Buzz Beach/California Cruiser but will only be offered on the higher-spec long-wheelbase van with 4Motion. We'll have more details once they're made public.
Source: VW Commercial Vehicles

        December vote would launch rulemaking to mirror barriers facing US providers abroad, with EU spectrum plans in the crosshairs
    
The US Federal Communications Commission (FCC) will vote in December on proposals [PDF] that would restrict foreign satellite operators in the same way their home countries restrict US providers. 
The telecoms regulator says the move is to ""ensure reciprocity and fair dealing"" in the rules governing satellite operators. The main aim appears to be hitting back at European efforts to build up the region's own space communications industries.
According to the FCC, the US has so far welcomed satellite systems licensed abroad on a level playing field, but that regulators elsewhere have begun holding US systems to different rules than they apply to their own domestic suppliers. 
""This has operated to the disadvantage of American satellite providers that are or may be burdened with regulatory and spectrum limitations that their competitors in foreign nations simply do not face,"" said FCC chairman Brendan Carr.
The FCC has singled out the EU's Digital Networks Act, which proposes harmonized connectivity rules across the bloc. A separate proposal would overhaul authorization for mobile satellite services (MSS) in the 2 GHz band after existing rights expire in May 2027.
Earlier this year, the FCC laid out some of its concerns, saying that EU-wide authorization of satellite spectrum would replace the national licensing by member states ""with which US satellite operators have become accustomed,"" and could require wholesale relicensing of any US systems operating in Europe.
""The FCC voting on foreign satellite companies is consistent with the Digital Networks Act, but is more of a response to the EU's decisions on how it plans to allocate the EU-wide 2 GHz spectrum,"" CCS Insight analyst Joe Gardiner told The Register.
""The EU outlined in its proposal that, for two-thirds of the spectrum, only operators based in the EU with EU ownership and aligned with the EU's strategic goals will be considered for the spectrum.
""This has locked out companies like SpaceX and Amazon Leo from attaining access to spectrum that is highly valuable for satellite direct-to-device services. The US government is looking to retaliate, to support the US constellations that would have attempted to win this EU-wide spectrum.""
Following an initial consultation in March, the FCC will vote in December on opening a rulemaking to reconsider how foreign-licensed satellite operators gain access to the US market.
""It is time for this regulatory asymmetry to end,"" Carr said, adding that the FCC now proposes to mirror any regulations that foreign nations apply to American satellite providers.
""If there are limits or restrictions on American providers operating abroad, then the notice proposes to apply those or similar regulatory restraints to satellite providers from those countries that are operating here.""
Britain is outside the EU, but does not escape the FCC's ire. The agency previously claimed the UK Space Agency allocates a significant portion of its budget, circa 75 percent in 2023/24, to European Space Agency (ESA) procurements, which US companies are not allowed to bid for as America is not a member of ESA.
British satellite operators could therefore face restrictions on access to the US market if the FCC adopts reciprocal measures. The UK's space industry generated £18.6 billion in income in 2022/23, according to the UK Space Agency. ®

I won't beat around the bush; the JBL Go 5 is the best cheap Bluetooth speaker I've ever tested. Compared to its predecessor - the JBL Go 4 - the new model remains impressively affordable (even if it's 5 bucks north in the US) and boasts improvements to connectivity, battery life, and waterproofing.
While bass won't reach the considerable highs of a larger party speaker like the JBL Xtreme 5 (see below), the overall soundstage is mighty impressive for such a tiny device. Battery life still isn't exceptional, sure, but if you're after a cheap portable speaker with an attractive design, look no further than the JBL Go 5.
What you're really getting here is an accomplished upgrade over the already excellent JBL Go 4. The JBL companion app works just as well here, opening up EQ adjustment and stereo pairing if you have another JBL speaker via Auracast. PlaytimeBoost can also provide a few more hours of listening, which you may need, given the speaker’s fairly limited battery life.
Like its predecessor, bass isn't perhaps as booming as we'd like given the brand's pedigree. However, I was still surprised by the overall energetic quality of the mix, particularly with those mids and trebles.
The JBL Go 5 is such a smart little speaker. It comes in multiple colorways and has some nice lighting effects, which is really impressive for a device on the more affordable side of the budget spectrum. Portability is a real win here, too, as the device can fit in a pocket or a bag with little issue. An improved IP68 waterproof rating also matches its larger brethren, making it highly resistant to water damage and general wear and tear.
At $54.95 / £39.99 (about AU$75), need we say more? This is incredible value for such an accomplished Bluetooth speaker. At this price, even if you're looking for something pricier and more feature-rich, I'd still handily recommend the Go 5 as a backup.

When I tried out the Edifier ES20, I really wasn’t expecting it to be as good as it is. After all, it’s a very cheap model, often available for less than £50 in the UK (it’s typically a bit more in the US, hence its position in the $50-$100 category). On top of that, it’s ultra-compact and hardly the most powerful model on the market. But it still performs fantastically.
During a test against rivals in this price category, I was wowed by the ES20’s clean, balanced audio performance. The detail it showcased in the mid-range was something the spectacular JBL Clip 5 and Ultimate Ears Wonderboom 4 speakers couldn’t quite match. It also has attractive lighting, great waterproofing, and a luxurious look that far surpassed my expectations in this price range.
The Edifier ES20 is equipped with so many tools that helped it to stand out over other options I tested in this price-range. Perhaps the most memorable was its in-built mic with integrated noise-cancelling technology. This means you can enjoy clear, hands-free calls without running for your phone. I also appreciated its strong 15-hour battery life, customizable lighting, and multi-speaker pairing options. It's just a shame about the limited EQ adjustment.
As I mentioned above, the mid-range performance of the ES20 is where it really shines. When comparing this to the Ultimate Ears Wonderboom 4, I was struck by the definition and tonal accuracy of guitars and vocals more than anything. You get natural-sounding treble too, which stays disciplined until you hit those really high volumes. Bass heads may prefer the weight of the Wonderboom 4, but the ES20 still offers a punchy and relatively clear low-end effort. Just don’t expect it to hit those deep dark notes in the sub-bass realm.
The star of the show, though, is the ES20’s design. I found that a number of my colleagues really loved the retro-inspired look of this Edifier speaker, with an appealing speaker grille protecting the driver and a faux leather material used for the outer casing and button controls. Lights positioned on the reverse side of the speaker can illuminate a wall behind it, increasing immersion in your favorite tunes. Oh, and it's also IP67 dust and waterproof, meaning it's as durable as it is beautiful.
If the ES20’s cost in the US were closer to its UK pricing, the ES20 would easily score a 5/5 in the value department. However, as it stands, it gets slightly marked down. I still think the ES20 is well-worth its sub-$90 list price, though, thanks to its surprising sound quality, striking design, and plentiful playtime.

The JBL Xtreme 5 is expensive, and as a relatively new product launching in 2026, it's going to hold its price quite well outside of major sales events. But it is worth pointing out that the premium you're paying here is worth the quality of the build, excellent software and big, booming sound. It’s actually unbelievable how long the Marshall Kilburn III can go for without needing a charge – 50 hours to be exact. For a model in its weight class, that’s an outrageous amount of playtime. This model can also be used to charge external devices like mobile phones and headphones, so you can share the power around with ease.
But this thing’s not only a leader in the battery life department – it’s also a top performer when it comes to audio. You’ll uncover phenomenally balanced, detailed, and impactful audio on the Kilburn III, and the Marshall app enables EQ tuning, so you can find your perfect sound.
The Kilburn III has just about everything you’d want from a modern Bluetooth speaker. The Marshall companion app opens up EQ adjustment, Auracast functionality, and battery preservation options. It also has placement compensation, which optimizes audio for your listening space. Of course, you’re also getting 50 hours of playtime, which is truly exemplary.
Even though it skips on higher-resolution Bluetooth audio codecs, this model is one of the best performers, sonically speaking, in its size class. You get seriously weighty, yet responsive bass output, fantastically defined mids, and expressive treble. True 360 stereophonic sound keeps your music sounding unreal from all angles, while deft instrument separation ensures a layered, nuanced listen.
I adore the amp-inspired look of the Kilburn III, which is impeccably crafted with metallic physical controls, hardy faux-leather, and a red velvet-infused carry strap. Its IP54 waterproofing doesn’t stack up too well against some rivals, but it's still splashproof and decently protected against dust.
The Marshall Kilburn III is pricier than its predecessor, but it really does feel worth its cost. With improved acoustics, a significantly upgraded look, and better waterproofing, it more than earns its higher price tag. Even against rivals, I’d consider it fairly priced; it's cheaper than the Bose SoundLink Max, for instance.

So you're looking to buy a Bluetooth speaker, but you're also slightly concerned about the drop in audio quality a Bluetooth connection can bring? In that case I can't recommend the new Sonos Play enough. Its Wi-Fi 6 capabilities mean you get to enjoy its rich, powerful audio with much less of a hit to overall sound quality.
And if you're already a Sonos head with some of the brand's products under your belt, multi-room technology is fully supported by the Sonos Play. That means devices including the Sonos Move 2 and a Sonos Era 100 can interact with the Sonos Play and form a home-wide sound system. Battery life is a huge improvement; at 24 hours, it'll last at least thrice as long as our previous sitter in this category — the Sonos Roam 2.
Wi-Fi streaming is of course the standout feature of the Sonos Play, but the device is also just so versatile. Expect robust connection options and fantastic streaming support. There's even a charging cradle bundled with the device. However, it is a bit cheeky that Sonos hasn't included a plug in the box, which may be understandably irksome to some.
Sound quality is the next big highlight of the Sonos Play. While it's not the most detailed sound you'll ever hear, and that lower end certainly lacks subtlety, you'll still be getting powerful, well-rounded audio.
Impressively portable, and that weight of 1.3kg means there's a satisfying heft to the Sonos Play. It's incredibly well-made from both a design and build quality standpoint, though I'm not especially keen on the overly subtle buttons lining the top of the speaker.
The Sonos Play is definitely pricier than the Sonos Roam 2 and certainly isn't easy on the wallet at $299 / £299. But it's hard to find similarly versatile products at this price point.

Our expert review:
Can debuts get much better than that of the Marshall Bromley 750? I'm really not too sure! Marshall’s first ever gigantic party speaker is fantastic-sounding, gorgeous-looking, and long-lasting beast, and I'd recommend it for any larger-scale event – remember, this thing is large enough that requires wheels and a handle, like a suitcase.
I found that this model could dish out almighty, yet regimented bass, while still giving mid-range sounds like guitars and vocals plenty of room to play. The gorgeous lights and amp-inspired design also help the Bromley to feel worth its fairly hefty price tag, and though its companion app could use some work, the speaker’s physical controls and connectivity options won me over.

There are a range of connectivity options too, like RCA ports for keyboards and turntables and XLR/6.35mm combo ports for microphones and electro-acoustic guitars – perfect for karaoke. The Marshall app is a little underequipped, however, meaning you’ll often have to rely on physical controls.

Sound character control also means you can stick with a more dynamic listen, or sacrifice a bit of fidelity for maximum loudness – you can also live somewhere in between. This works very well, and believe me, if you want to turn it up to 11 here, you can!

When I reviewed the Marshall Bromley 750, I found myself comparing it to the recently released JBL PartyBox. See, that model's priced very similarly to Marshall’s party debut, but it's got a significantly higher power output. If you want raw might, that one may well offer you better bang for your buck. Still, the ultra-premium build of the Bromley 750, paired with its great sound and repairability, give it great long-term value.

I have personally tested every single Bluetooth speaker listed in this guide, including a number that didn't quite make the cut – those are listed above. In order to build this guide, I spent multiple weeks testing a whole lot of models, comparing a load of rivals on aspects like sound quality, features, and design. On top of this, I've been consistently reviewing Bluetooth speakers and other related audio gear for more than a year and a half here at TechRadar.
Anyway, what does the actual testing process look (and sound) like? Well, first of all, I make sure I'm using Tidal to listen to music, thanks to its support for quality up to 24-bit and 192kHz, so I'm started with the best possible source. In addition, I make sure to run through the TechRadar testing playlist, which features tracks from a number of genres, like Classical, Electronic, Jazz, and Rock.
Of course, I want to have a bit of fun while doing this, so I always bump a few tracks from my personal music library too. I listen to a lot of house music and jazz funk fusion, but my playlists feature a range of tunes that help me to judge dynamics, tonal accuracy, and detail right across the frequency range.
I always test out audio gear in the music testing space we have here on TechRadar – a quiet, acoustically treated and controlled environment for judging the quality of audio gear. However, I do try to take speakers into other environments too, including my home, the office, outdoor spaces, and sometimes even the shower (if that IP rating is high enough, of course).
On top of this, I'll make sure to test performance across different connectivity methods. Sometimes a speaker won't showcase its full potential unless its making use of a wired connection. Maybe it needs to be plugged into a power source to hit its maximum power output. I'll try whatever is necessary to hear the full potential of each unit I try. This includes making use of higher resolution codecs, like aptX Adaptive, which require a compatible source device or USB-C dongle.
If a Bluetooth speaker has a companion app, I'll also ensure that it's downloaded (I have so many my phone is running out of storage). Then, I'll exhaust every option, from EQ adjustment through to customizable lighting or battery preservation modes.
Keen to learn more about how we review audio gear and more at TechRadar? Make sure you head on over to our 'how we test' guide.
 



Let's start with the most important question first. To choose the perfect Bluetooth speaker, you'll have to ask yourself a few questions. What do you want to use it for? Personal listening? Parties? Playing music in the shower? Some speakers might not have the raw power needed for parties, and some may not have the waterproofing required for mid-scrub listening, so consider the amount of power and protection you'll need, for instance.
It's also important to think about size. Do you want a mini speaker for dropping tunes in your bedroom? Do you want a behemoth that can get everybody moving at an outdoor gathering? Consider how much portability and room you need.
And it goes without saying, but set yourself a budget. Good quality audio really doesn't have to cost an arm and a leg. Of course, the sub-$10 speakers on Amazon should probably be avoided, but there are a number of options under $50 that can serve you well.
There are a lot of great waterproof speakers, but I can run through a few top choices. For a smaller model, I recommend the Edifier ES20 or the Ultimate Ears Wonderboom 4. These are both IP67-rated and each sound great.For a mid-sized option, the JBL Flip 7 or JBL Charge 6 are exemplary choices. We scored both models five stars, and their IP68 dust and waterproofing is out of this world.
Meanwhile, those seeking out a larger model will be best off with the Bose SoundLink Max or the Tribit Stormbox Blast 2. These both combine IP67 protection against the elements with seriously hefty audio output.
This comes down to two main things. First, budget; second, personal preference. JBL typically offers more budget-friendly audio gear than Bose, but you can still generally expect a terrific standard of sound and build quality. There's also a trademark look to a JBL speaker. It's rugged, outdoorsy, and often cylindrical. Maybe you dig that, maybe you don't!
Bose, for many, is synonymous with luxury. And that makes a lot of sense, especially given that the Bose SoundLink Max made the cut as my top premium pick. You often get impressive levels of sonic detail and a spectacular finish from Bose speakers. I'd argue that some models don't offer the spectacular value that JBL do, but if you've got the cash and want to hear some finer details, it may well be worth the additional spend.
A lot of the time, wireless speakers – those capable of streaming over Wi-Fi – are better equipped for home listening, often featuring a less rugged or portable design. But that comes with the benefit of playing music over Wi-Fi, which often sounds better than Bluetooth streaming.
That's because more data is typically lost during a Bluetooth stream, though some higher-quality codecs can retain a tad more detail. But the best Bluetooth speakers still sound great, and they can often offer superior portability and waterproofing among other things.
Some speakers can do both – see the Sonos Roam 2. If you're going to be using a speaker almost exclusively at home, indoors, then I'd recommending taking a peek at our best wireless speakers guide. Otherwise, one of the options on this list will certainly suit your needs.
 



In this context, frequency response refers to the range of frequencies that a speaker can reproduce. For example, if a speaker's frequency response is 20Hz-20kHz, that suggests that it is capable of replicating all sounds within the average human's range of hearing.
Audio codecs compress and encode audio signals, which are transmitted wirelessly from one device to another. These are then decoded by another device, and delivered to you, the listener. 
Allow me to provide an example. Say you're playing music via Spotify on the Edifier ES20 speaker, which is connected to your Android phone. The audio file – playing from Spotify – would then be compressed and re-encoded by your phone with the SBC codec, before being decoded by the Edifier speaker and played to you.
SBC is the most basic codec, and the vast majority of Bluetooth devices support it. AAC offers better sound quality than SBC, and can potentially transmit audio without compressing or re-encoding it, if the source music file is AAC, if both the speaker and your source device support AAC transmission, and if the file isn't too high-quality for Bluetooth's bandwidth. 
There are some more fancy codecs to uncover that audiophiles will likely appreciate. 
For instance, LDAC – Sony's proprietary coding tech – and aptX codecs, like aptX Adaptive, can open you up to higher-resolution Bluetooth audio. A lot of modern Android devices support LDAC, but not as many phones and tablets support aptX Adaptive. 
Crucially, you'll need to make sure your source device and Bluetooth speaker both support the codec you want to make use of. For instance, the Xiaomi 17 Pro Max and the Bose SoundLink Plus each support aptX Adaptive. Make sure the streaming service you use also enables higher-resolution streaming. Tidal, for instance, can open up streaming in 24-bit, 192 kHz, so you'll be able to hear those additional details that aptX Adaptive can uncover.
A Bluetooth speaker doesn't necessarily need to support LDAC or aptX to sound good – the JBL and Marshall speakers on this list, for example, offer impressive sound without them. But it can be a nice touch for those listeners who want a more intricate, revealing listen.
OK, this one's a little less tech intensive! IP ratings refer to the dust and water protection a product has. After the letters IP, you'll find two additional characters. The first indicates its dustproof rating, while the second shows its waterproof rating.
For example, if a Bluetooth speaker is IP67-rated, then that tells us two specific things. Firstly, the '6' shows that the model is dust-tight, and completely protected against dust ingress. Meanwhile, the '7' means that the speaker is waterproof, and can safely be submerged under a meter of freshwater for up to 30 minutes.
When you buy a new Bluetooth speaker, you should always check its IP rating. If it's IPX7 rated, for example, that means it does not have a dust resistance guarantee, but is safe to be submerged underwater. If it is IP54 rated, say, that means that it's partially dust-proof, but only splashproof, rather than fully waterproof.
If you're buying a more recently released Bluetooth speaker, like the ones in my guide, then my honest answer to this question is: not much.
All modern phones can connect to the speakers in this guide, though its worth noting that only speakers with the Bluetooth 5.2 version or later support the LC3 codec, which is used for Auracast streams. 
A lot of the time, yes. However, speakers can only generally be paired with specific models – often, multiple units of the same model. One brand that has a slightly more expansive approach is JBL, which lets you connect a wide variety of its speakers together, so long as they support Auracast. You may need two of the exact same model for stereo pairing, though.

Regain Control Of Google Ads: Better Tracking, Clearer Proof Of ROI
WordPress keeps losing share of websites, and no other platform is gaining much. Here's what that means for SEO audits and site pitches.
WordPress is in use on 38.4% of all websites, according to W3Techs, Nov. 23. A year prior, WordPress was being used on 43.2% of all websites. In that time, the share of websites using none of the content management systems tracked by W3Techs grew 4.4 percentage points, from 28.6% to 33.0%.
Shopify improved 0.6 points over the year; Wix and Squarespace barely changed. For sites with no detected CMS, neither a WordPress SEO plugin nor another platform’s defaults can be assumed to handle titles, canonical tags and sitemaps.
There are more than 20 million websites included in W3Techs’ survey. The data is updated each day. Shelley Walsh’s November CMS market share update used the survey’s Nov. 13 figures. At that time, WordPress had dropped to 39.2% of all websites.
WordPress’s percentage of all sites has fallen 1.1 points so far in November, which is larger than its fall in any single complete month this year, per the survey’s month-by-month data.
WordPress’ declines have been larger since August. For the first seven months of the year, WordPress fell less than 0.5 points each month. It then dropped 0.5 points a month in August and September, and 0.7 points in October.
Roger Montti reported that WordPress fell for the sixth consecutive month as of May, when it was at 41.9%.
The gap between WordPress and sites with no detected CMS narrowed from 14.6 percentage points to 5.4 points over the period shown below.
WordPress Fell As The Share With No Detected CMS Rose
Share of all websites in W3Techs’ survey, Nov. 1, 2025 to Nov. 23, 2026.
WordPress fell 4.8 points over the year. Shopify rose from 4.9% to 5.5% of all websites and Wix from 4.1% to 4.2%, while Squarespace held steady and Joomla and Drupal slipped. The share of sites with no detected CMS rose 4.4 points over the same year.
Wix grows on the survey’s market share measure, which counts only sites with a CMS W3Techs detects. That pool gets smaller as the share with no detected CMS grows. By that count, WordPress fell from 60.5% to 57.3%, while Wix rose from 5.7% to 6.2% and Shopify rose too. Most of Wix’s gain comes from the smaller pool, since its share of all websites barely changed.
In March, I noted that the share of sites with no detected CMS stood at 28.9%, close to its low after years of decline. Since February, it has risen each month.
W3Techs now lists Lovable as a CMS, describing it as “an AI-powered platform for building websites”. It lists Lovable at 0.5% of total websites, about 0.1 point below Drupal’s current 0.6%.
The monthly table provided by W3Techs does not show any Lovable data prior to November.
In her report, Shelley said that she is most interested in how AI and vibecoding may influence CMS platforms, and whether there will be more non-CMS based websites. Now that W3Techs has Lovable on its list, her first question has a number, at least for one AI builder.
Nearly one-third of websites in W3Techs’ survey are now using none of the CMSs it tracks. That means you can’t count on a WordPress SEO plugin, or another platform’s defaults, to set or manage title tags, canonical tags, robots meta directives, or sitemaps on such websites. An audit or pitch will need to determine what handles them on these websites, or if nothing does.
The key figure to keep an eye on is the share of sites running none of the CMSs tracked by W3Techs, which has risen 1.5 points since Oct. 1. The Dec. 1 numbers will help show whether that pace continues.

67,2026-11-24,"Before Jensen Huang became the face of Nvidia, he and Lori Huang met as engineering lab partners, where he offered to let her look at his homework.
""That was my way of asking a girl out for a date,"" he told the Computer History Museum in a 2024 interview.
Their decadeslong partnership has since unfolded largely out of public view, even as Jensen's professional profile grew into one of the most prominent in tech.
On November 8, Huang admitted that he had been unfaithful to his wife, shortly after The New York Times reported on his close relationship with Alice Huang, an executive at the Singaporean data center company Megaspeed.
The Times reported that US intelligence officials believed Alice Huang had been passing information to Beijing. China has denied the espionage allegations.
By November 12, Jensen Huang was out of Nvidia and was alleged to have also had an ""intimate relationship"" with an unnamed second Nvidia employee, Reuters reported.
On Monday, he returned as Nvidia's CEO, less than two weeks after he formally stepped down amid the scandals.
Here's a timeline of his relationship with his wife, Lori.
More than a decade before Huang cofounded Nvidia, he and then-Lori Mills met as electrical-engineering lab partners in the early 1980s at Oregon State University in Corvallis, Oregon.
Jensen was 17 at the time — he'd skipped two grades — and Lori was 19. Both earned Bachelor of Science degrees in electrical engineering from the university.
""I was fortunate enough to be in the same lab team as her,"" he told the Computer History Museum in the 2024 interview.
He said he spent six months getting Lori to do homework with him every weekend.
""That was my way of asking a girl out for a date, I guess,"" he told the museum.
""My pickup line was, 'You want to see my homework?'"" he said. ""I knew that that was my superpower. I was good at homework.""
It worked, and they got together.
He added, ""Choosing electrical engineering put the two of us on the same path, and we've been together ever since.""
In 2025's ""The Thinking Machine,"" author Stephen Witt writes that Jensen proposed to Lori at Advanced Micro Devices' Christmas party at Moscone Center in San Francisco.
Following the proposal, the two drove toward Oregon, crashed on an icy road near the California-Oregon border, and survived. Huang needed stitches and a neck brace after the crash, Witt wrote.
Huang briefly recalled the crash during a 2023 interview on the ""Acquired"" podcast, saying they totaled his ""brand new"" Toyota Supra shortly after getting engaged.
""We were this close to the end,"" he said.
The two were married for eight years before Jensen Huang co-founded Nvidia in 1993 with Chris Malachowsky and Curtis Priem.
He has said publicly that he owes much of his success to the support Lori has given him over the years.
""It's good to have someone in your life who unconditionally believes in you, who believes you're doing your best,"" he told the Oregon State College of Engineering in a 2014 interview.
""You need people like that, and Lori's always been that person for me— ever since I was 18, she was there believing in me and knowing I could do it.""
Witt wrote in ""The Thinking Machine"" that Lori Huang, a microchip engineer at Silicon Graphics, left her career to raise their two children shortly before Nvidia was founded.
Spencer and Madison Huang both now work at the company.
Spencer is a director of product management in Nvidia's robotics business, leading product strategy for software used in robot simulation, learning, synthetic data, and physical AI, according to his LinkedIn profile.
He joined the company in 2022 after earning a technical MBA focused on AI from NYU.
Madison is Nvidia's senior director of product and technical marketing for its physical-AI platforms, including Omniverse, Cosmos, and Isaac, according to the company's website.
Before joining Nvidia in 2020, she worked in luxury-industry marketing at LVMH and earned an MBA in marketing and strategy from London Business School.
The private family foundation is led by Lori as president, while Jensen serves as its secretary and CFO.
Its first tax filing listed $13,000 in assets and no grants. By the end of 2024, as Nvidia’s value surged, the foundation reported more than $9.1 billion in net assets, according to its tax filings.
In 2024, it donated $126.3 million, including about $30.5 million to Oregon State University, while about $82.5 million went to Schwab Charitable, a donor-advised fund sponsor.
In 2009, they donated $2.5 million to Oregon State University’s Kelley Engineering Center, where an atrium was named in their honor, the university reported.
Jensen Huang delivered a keynote speech at the building’s opening and highlighted the moment he was paired with Lori as an engineering lab partner. ""That was the most important, single event of OSU, and of my life,"" he said.
Their donations became more visible in 2022, when they gave Oregon State $50 million for the Jen-Hsun and Lori Huang Collaborative Innovation Complex, an AI and engineering research center on the Corvallis campus, the university reported.
The couple said the school was where they discovered their love for computer science and engineering.
In April 2026, they attended the Breakthrough Prize ceremony at Santa Monica's Barker Hangar, an annual gathering often described as the ""Oscars of science.""
Months later, they were among the tech-world power players invited to the White House state dinner for China's leader Xi Jinping, where they joined leaders including Elon Musk, Sam Altman, and Tim Cook.
In a letter sent to Nvidia's staff on November 8, the billionaire CEO and cofounder apologized for his unfaithfulness.
""I owe my family and all of you an apology,"" he wrote. ""I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did.""
The admission triggered an independent investigation, his resignation on November 12, a total loss of about $1 trillion in market value, and his eventual return to the company as CEO on November 23.
Jensen Huang has said that he's ""gonna be a better CEO the second time around,"" according to a person who attended his arrival at the company's headquarters.
Lori Huang has not publicly commented on her husband's infidelity.
Business Insider reached out to Nvidia, Lori Huang, and the Jen-Hsun and Lori Huang Foundation for comment.

China Southern Airlines says its subsidiary, Xiamen Airlines, has signed an agreement with Boeing to buy 50 737-8 aircraft at a list price of about $7.4bn, according to a stock exchange filing.
The purchase agreement was filed with the stock exchange on Tuesday.
The actual purchase price would be lower than the list price as Boeing had offered Xiamen Airlines a “significant discount”, China Southern said in the filing. Large discounts are standard practice when airlines place sizeable orders with planemakers.
The order follows a commitment for 200 jets that China made during a summit between United States President Donald Trump and Chinese leader Xi Jinping in Beijing in May. The filing did not say whether this order is part of that commitment.
Boeing had not won a major order from China in almost a decade. Last year China reportedly prohibited its airlines from taking deliveries of Boeing jets. It also paused purchases of aircraft-related equipment and parts from US companies in the midst of a trade war, after Trump unleashed a barrage of tariffs on Beijing.
US Trade Representative Jamieson Greer said in September that “there are about 140 [jetliner orders] that are in a good state”, with another 10 orders being finalised.
Trump said on Monday that he thinks China will buy another 250 Boeing airplanes. He gave no details, and Chinese officials have not confirmed any further purchases.
The planes in the Xiamen agreement are scheduled for delivery in batches between 2031 and 2034, China Southern said in the filing, adding that the deal is subject to approval from the company’s shareholders and relevant Chinese government authorities.
China Southern said the order would help Xiamen Airlines seize opportunities arising from its move to the new Xiamen Xiang’an international airport, support the construction of an aviation hub in Fujian province and optimise its fleet structure.
Xiamen Airlines, historically an all-Boeing operator, ended a nearly four-decade-long all-Boeing fleet in early 2023 when it took delivery of its first Airbus A321neo under an operating lease.
In April, China Southern and Xiamen Airlines also agreed to buy 137 Airbus A320neo family aircraft.









Follow Al Jazeera English:"
70,2026-11-24,"The Linux community has made massive strides in getting Windows apps working on Linux, to the point where we can just run anything we want through Proton and see what happens.
But what if you wanted to use macOS apps instead? Well, instead of using virtual machines or reverse engineering, one project believes it has the solution: stick the entire macOS kernel into Linux and let it handle it all.
As spotted by Linuxiac, XNU Linux aims to make it easier to run macOS apps on your Linux machine. The best part is, it doesn't need to use any translation or hypervisor tech to achieve that goal; instead, it takes XNU, an open-source kernel for macOS from Apple, and stuffs it into an arm64 Linux kernel. This allows the macOS and Linux processes to run natively next to one another on the same CPU.
It seems to be making pretty big strides already; in fact, its creator, Heyang Zhou, claims they booted macOS 27.2.1 to the desktop using a stock Ubuntu install. You can check it out in the screenshot above.
The system requirements are pretty tight; it's only for ARM64 devices with Pointer Authentication (PAC) and 4K pages. It has only been tested on bare-metal Nvidia DGX Spark hardware and Apple Silicon Macs that are M1 or newer (running Linux inside a VM via krunkit or tart, though XNU still runs directly inside Linux without a second hypervisor), and you need your own copy of macOS.
If you'd still like to give it a try, head over to the XNU Linux GitHub page and download it from there. Just remember it's still an experiment, so be ready for any and all weirdness that arises when using new tech.
What could go wrong?

Doctor Victor von Doom's ego and conviction in his own superiority are as enormous as the universe, but in his cold heart, he does respect some of the ""foolish"" heroes who oppose him. Doom unconditionally loves his goddaughter, Valeria Richards of the Fantastic Four. He is frenemies with Namor the Sub-Mariner, and also maintains a friendly rivalry with his fellow sorcerer Doctor Stephen Strange; their dynamic lacks the bitterness that defines Doom's feud with Reed Richards. Doom also acknowledges Captain America as a paragon of his chosen ideals, and sees even Spider-Man's puerility as if he's a court jester, the one willing to tell a king what no one else will.
But there's one hero that Doom most consistently views and addresses as an equal, and that's Ororo Monroe, Storm, of the X-Men. Storm is easily one of the strongest X-Men; she can control the weather itself and is a self-styled ""goddess."" Even Doom concedes her that description. Unusually for Doom, he rarely tries to downplay Storm's power next to his own. Ororo's strength intrigues him — as does her beauty. 
Now, Doom isn't the kind of man to act on romantic whimsy. ""Doom needs no one"" is one of his self-affirming catchphrases, and he once drove away his childhood sweetheart Valeria to prioritize his quest for knowledge and power. Part of Doom's self-inflicted misery is choosing to believe that no woman could ever love a man with a scarred face like his, yet another reason Doom hides behind a mask: ""How could she... when even I am driven to madness by the sight!"" But even so, one feels Doom would revel in the fantasy of Storm sitting by his side. It all goes back to their first meeting in ""Uncanny X-Men"" #145 by Chris Claremont and Dave Cockrum.
""Uncanny X-Men"" issues #145-147 comprise a short arc where the twisted gamesman Arcade teams up with Doom to destroy the X-Men. In #145, Storm shows up at Doom's castle... and he welcomes her as an honored guest, treating her to a feast while the other X-Men are attacked by mecha-armored guards. Storm herself is charmed by Doom, thinking he is ""a fascinating man,"" and he reciprocates.
""Storm seems drawn to me, as I am to her. Lioness to lion. Queen to king. She does not fear me. She should. She will.""
Indeed, Doom soon defeats the X-Men, Storm included, as he dismisses them all as simple ""children"" next to him. While the others are thrown into deathtraps, Doom — charmed as he was by Storm — transmutes the still conscious Ororo into a chrome statue, while creating a robot in Storm's image to serve him. For Ororo, who is claustrophobic, being trapped inside her own body is a living hell. Once freed, she lives up to her name and unfurls a terrifying storm. Echoing Doom's own words, Storm declares: ""Am I not beautiful, Lord Doom? And terrible? Do you not fear me? You should. You shall.""
Normally, this would be a bungled first impression. But after surrendering Arcade, Doom apologizes to Storm for his behavior, suggesting his infatuation with her was real: ""I would hope that we might begin anew. You are a fascinating woman. I should like to know you better."" Ororo grants him the grace of a clean slate.
The events of this arc are referenced almost any time Doom and Storm meet, such as in issue #4 of Storm's 2024 solo series, where Doom hosts her in Latveria. He's not alone among villains in his view of Storm, either.
Read any of Chris Claremont's writing, including the hundreds of ""X-Men"" stories to his name, and it's easy to tell that he's got a thing for powerful women. No ""X-Men"" character more consistently shows that than Storm, the strongest of the team and their frequent leader. It often feels like Claremont wrote his own infatuation with the character into the text; ""powerful villain tries to take Storm as theirs"" is a stock Claremont ""X-Men"" plot, and not just with Doom.
Claremont and Bill Sienkiewicz's ""Uncanny X-Men"" #159 sees Dracula himself try to turn Storm into his bride. (New comic series ""Midnight X-Men"" takes place in a timeline where he succeeded, and Storm reigns as a vampire queen.)
An attraction to Storm binds Doom and Dracula... or does it? Shortly after Doom's guest appearance in ""X-Men,"" writer-artist John Byrne took over ""Fantastic Four."" Byrne had made his name drawing ""X-Men,"" but ultimately left due to creative differences with Claremont. A lingering mark of those differences shows up in ""Fantastic Four"" #258. Byrne, apparently dissatisfied with how Claremont wrote Doom, suggests the Doom who teamed up with Arcade was one of Doom's robotic duplicates, or Doombots.
Now, in the ""X-Men"" issues themselves, there's no hint this isn't the real Doom. Years later, in Al Ewing and Stefano Caselli's ""S.W.O.R.D."" #7, Storm hosts Doom for dinner, and their conversation is loaded with references to ""X-Men"" #145. Doom blames his unbecoming behavior of attacking Storm, his guest, as a Doombot's faulty programming (but he would, wouldn't he?). Across the conversation, Doom demonstrates his interest in Storm is all too real, to the point Ororo tells him: ""Don't flirt, Victor. It's unbecoming."" Except, Doom would likely argue, with an exceptional woman like her."

73,2026-11-24,"Sony’s iconic Walkman headphones are back in the USB-C MDR-3R and, after quickly selling out on launch day, they’ve now been restocked. And, after using them, I can see why they sold out so quickly. 
The MDR-3R are Sony’s revival of the wired over-ear headphones that many used with a classic Walkman player. The retro style still looks great in 2026, but using an original pair is a bit of a headache in today’s age of headphone jack-less smartphones. So it was nice to see Sony give this a revival with USB-C.
And, yeah, they live up to the hype.
Update 11/24: After we first published our hands-on with MDR-3R last week, Sony’s USB-C headphones sold out pretty quickly again. As of today, though, they’re back in stock and still shipping in early December – don’t miss out!
While the style won’t be for everyone, I think these work brilliantly. The sound quality is excellent and having a pair of headphones that doesn’t fully enclose my ears is pretty refreshing. There’s no noise cancellation, but with open-ear earbuds becoming more popular, I actually really like these as a similar solution (though high volumes will definitely be heard by those around you). I’m not fully cut off from the world around me, but I’m also able to get good sound quality. Lows are the weakness here, with unimpressive bass drops, but the sound quality is crisp and clear and pretty neutral, and the open-ish style makes it feel like the room around me is filled with music.
The physical hardware is great. While a plastic and metal build is objectively not as comfy as a premium pair of ANC headphones, the extremely light weight helps MDR-3R become an afterthought. You simply forget you’re wearing them after a few minutes.
The earpads are swappable, with orange and blue options included in the box. 
As for the USB-C cable, it’s fully plug-and-play. No app needed and it worked across every device I tested (including Pixel, which doesn’t work with all USB-C audio output). I do wish the cable was perhaps a few inches longer, but it’s long enough to wear the headphones with my phone in my front pocket. There’s a remote along the cable for play/pause and volume adjustments, though I think it’s a little too high on the cable.
For $59.99, these are a fun pair of USB-C headphones to have on hand. They sound good, work great, and don’t cost an arm and a leg. I can see why they sold out so quickly on launch day – in just 10 hours, Sony told me. 
Sony MDR-3R are back in stock now and shipping in early December. If you want a pair, I say don’t wait. They’re well worth at least trying if you can get them for retail price – please don’t give in to the scalpers selling these at double, they’re not worth that.

74,2026-11-24,"The BleeqUp Rover is the brand’s most capable AI sports glasses to date, stepping up from the Ranger with a longer feature list, more onboard storage, and a suite of AI-powered tools that go well beyond simple hands-free recording. 
Priced at $459.00, it sits at the premium end of the smart glasses market. Its feature set is clearly intended to justify that price. This is not a pair of sunglasses with a camera bolted on. 
BleeqUp has built the Rover around the idea that your eyewear should function as an active sports companion: capturing footage, monitoring your body, alerting you to hazards, and helping you edit and share the results, all without stopping the ride.
In this review we cover the Rover’s design and build, camera performance, audio quality, battery life, AI features and software, and whether the price is justified for the cyclist, trail runner, or outdoor adventurer it’s clearly aimed at.
The Rover is built on a TR90 frame, a thermoplastic material commonly used in performance eyewear because it is lightweight and flexible. The whole package comes in at 53g, which is heavier than a standard pair of sport sunglasses but lighter than it sounds when you factor in everything packed inside. After a few minutes on the face, most users report forgetting it’s there.
The fit system is thoughtful. A three-axis adjustable nose pad lets you dial in the fit across different face shapes, which matters more here than on a standard pair of glasses because a poor fit affects both comfort and camera angle. The temples measure 125.27mm in length and the overall frame width sits at 142mm, making this a medium-to-large fit. Those with narrower faces may want to try before committing.
IP65 water resistance is a meaningful spec for an outdoor product. That rating means the Rover can handle dust ingress and direct water jets, so it is designed to stand up to rain and sweat. It is not rated for submersion, so open-water swimming is out, but everything short of that should be fine.
Lens options include ZEISS UV400 glass in gold or silver tint, with optional photochromic lenses (clear-to-light-grey or clear-to-dark-red) available as upgrades. The ZEISS branding is a useful selling point, although lens clarity ultimately needs to be judged in use. Each lens weighs 12g and carries a Category 2 rating with 18–43% light transmission, which covers most outdoor conditions but may feel slightly dark in overcast or low-light environments.
The included prescription frame insert is a welcome addition for riders who need corrective lenses, and BleeqUp offers custom single-vision lenses cut to your prescription directly through the site.
The camera is the centerpiece of the Rover, and it’s where the hardware investment is most visible. The 16MP sensor shoots stills at 4656 x 3496 pixels and supports video at 1080p30, 1080p60, 3K30, and 3K60. Both landscape (16:9) and portrait (9:16) aspect ratios are supported, which is a practical addition for anyone creating content for social platforms.
The standout hardware feature is the 3-level tilt camera lens, adjustable to -10°, 0°, or 15°. This sounds minor until you’ve used glasses that spend an entire ride pointing at the road surface. The -10° setting accommodates a helmet brim or cap peak; 0° gives a natural eye-level perspective; 15° is designed for the forward-lean posture of road cycling. It’s a simple mechanical adjustment that makes a real difference in usable footage.
Electronic Image Stabilization (EIS) is active across all recording modes and does a solid job smoothing out the vibration from road surfaces and trail riding. Reviewers note that walking and moderate cycling footage looks clean, though rougher terrain at speed will still show some residual shake because electronic stabilization cannot eliminate every bump.
The 120° field of view is wide enough to capture genuine environmental context without the fish-eye distortion that plagues ultra-wide action cameras. Real-time HDR video and a dedicated Night Mode round out the feature set. HDR improves highlight and shadow detail in high-contrast outdoor scenes, while Night Mode makes low-light recording possible, although a small sensor will still struggle in darker scenes.
One honest limitation worth noting: clip length is capped at one minute for 3K60 and 1080p60, five minutes for 3K30, with no limit at 1080p30. Clips save automatically and you can start a new recording immediately, but it’s a workflow consideration for anyone expecting unbroken long-form footage at the highest quality settings. Loop Recording is available as an alternative, continuously overwriting older footage when the 64GB of onboard storage fills up. That can be useful for incident recording on commutes.
The Rover uses an open-ear speaker system with two full-range drivers positioned near the ears without sealing the ear canal. This is a deliberate design choice for outdoor use, where situational awareness matters. You can hear your navigation, music, or a group walkie-talkie call while still picking up traffic, other riders, and your surroundings.
The trade-off is exactly what you’d expect: without a seal, bass response is limited and maximum volume is modest. At low to moderate speeds, audio is clear and usable. Above roughly 35 km/h, wind noise becomes the dominant sound in your ears. BleeqUp lists 35 km/h as the threshold for effective wind noise reduction, though results will depend on conditions. At highway cycling speeds or on fast descents, the audio experience degrades noticeably.
The 5-microphone array is more impressive than the speaker output. Five mics with noise reduction means voice commands register reliably even in wind, and call quality for the other party is reported as clear. The walkie-talkie function, handled through the BleeqUp app, works in real time and is genuinely useful for group rides. Users can create a channel and invite friends through the app.
One recurring note from users: recorded audio from the onboard microphones can sound thin and low in volume on playback. This is a common limitation of mic arrays built into eyewear frames, where the microphones are necessarily small and positioned away from the mouth. For action footage where ambient sound is the goal, it’s acceptable. For vlogging or commentary-style recording, it may fall short of expectations.
The Rover’s onboard 260 mAh battery is the most significant practical constraint of the product. BleeqUp publishes the following continuous-use figures:
The Power Plus accessory module adds a 1,600 mAh external battery that extends those figures to:
For short rides, commutes, or casual use, the internal battery is sufficient. For anything longer than an hour of active recording, such as a long cycling event, an all-day trail run, or a multi-stage trip, the Power Plus becomes much more important. Multiple reviewers make this point directly, and it’s fair to say BleeqUp has designed the Rover with the expectation that serious users will bundle the accessory.
Charging time for the internal battery is one hour via the included magnetic cable. The proprietary magnetic connector is a minor inconvenience because replacing a lost cable may be less straightforward than using a standard USB-C cord, although the connector simplifies docking. The bundle kit, which pairs the Rover with the Power Plus and a Bluetooth Controller, starts at $519, saving $38 versus buying separately.
The AI feature set is where the Rover separates itself from every other pair of camera glasses on the market, and it’s clear this is where BleeqUp has invested the most development effort.
AI Hazard Detection uses the camera feed and GPS data to proactively provide spoken alerts about upcoming climbs, sharp descents, tight corners, and changing weather conditions. It also monitors for sudden braking events and automatically triggers emergency recording, capturing a 10-second pre-event buffer alongside post-event footage. For urban commuters worried about close passes, or trail riders on unfamiliar routes, this is genuinely useful rather than a gimmick.
Breathing Monitoring & Analysis uses the onboard sensors to track your breathing pattern during exercise and alerts you when irregularities are detected. It’s a wellness feature rather than a medical one, but it adds a layer of passive health awareness that no competing product currently offers at this price.
On the content side, AI Highlight Detection automatically identifies and saves the best moments from your footage. AI Clips Search lets you find a specific moment by describing it verbally rather than scrubbing through video. One-Tap AI Editing assembles highlights into a shareable video without any manual editing. AI Video Roadbook overlays your route data onto footage to create a visual journey summary. AI Master Motion and AI Master Remix use still photos to generate dynamic video content with cinematic angles. The usefulness of these effects will depend on how often owners share edited clips.
Voice Command runs through all of these features, letting you trigger recording, search clips, ask sports questions, and request an activity recap without touching the glasses. The AI Sports Q&A function answers sports-related questions on demand, and AI Activity Recap delivers a post-workout summary when you finish.
The app took some users a couple of evenings to fully figure out, and BleeqUp acknowledges this with tutorial content. Once learned, the interface is described as slick and well-integrated. Note that Proactive Safety Alerts are currently available in English only.
BleeqUp backs the Rover with a one-year warranty (subject to local laws), a 30-day no-questions return policy with a full refund, and what it describes as lifetime customer support via email and phone. Customer service operates Sunday through Friday during business hours. The warranty is standard for the category. It is neither an unusual advantage nor an obvious drawback.
At $459.00, the Rover is priced above entry-level action cameras and significantly above standard sport sunglasses. The comparison it invites is with dedicated action cameras like the GoPro HERO series or competitors in the smart glasses space. Against a standalone action camera, the Rover trades some raw image quality for the hands-free convenience and the AI feature layer. Against other smart glasses, it offers meaningfully better optics (ZEISS glass), more storage (64GB vs. the Ranger’s 32GB), and a substantially deeper AI toolkit.
The honest framing is this: $459 for glasses alone is a significant ask. The more complete experience with the Rover, Power Plus, and Bluetooth Controller runs $519. For a casual user who wants to occasionally record a ride, that’s hard to justify. For a cyclist or outdoor athlete who records regularly, values situational awareness, and will actually use the AI editing tools, the value proposition is considerably stronger.
Awarded to products with an average rating of 3.75 stars or higher, the AndroidGuys Smart Pick recognizes a balance of quality, performance, and value. 
Products with this distinction deserve to be on your short list of purchase candidates.
The BleeqUp Rover is the most capable AI sports glasses we’ve seen at this price point, and it earns that position through genuine engineering rather than spec-sheet padding. The 3-level tilt camera, 3K60 HDR video, ZEISS optics, and layered AI feature set add up to something that functions as a real sports tool rather than a novelty. The open-ear audio works well within its limits, and the safety features, including emergency recording with pre-event buffering, could also prove useful during unexpected incidents.
The caveats are real: the one-hour internal battery means the Power Plus is a near-essential accessory for anyone doing longer sessions, the proprietary charging cable is a vulnerability, recorded audio can sound thin on playback, and the app has a learning curve. None of these are dealbreakers, but they’re worth knowing before you buy.
Who should buy it: cyclists, trail runners, and outdoor athletes who record regularly, want hands-free AI-assisted editing, and value situational awareness over the isolation of traditional earbuds. If you’re looking for the most complete version of the experience, budget for the $519 bundle from the start.
The BleeqUp Rover brings an unusually broad feature set to sports eyewear, pairing an adjustable 3K camera with open-ear audio, weather resistance, and AI-assisted activity and editing tools. Its camera positioning and hands-free controls are particularly useful for cyclists and outdoor athletes who want to capture a ride without mounting a separate camera. The trade-offs are substantial: about an hour of onboard video recording, limitations from wind and small microphones, a proprietary charging cable, and a companion app that takes time to learn. At $459 for the glasses or $519 for the bundle with Power Plus and a controller, it makes the most sense for frequent riders who will use the extra features, not someone who only occasionally records a short outing.

76,2026-11-24,"Happy Tuesday! It's November 24, 2026, and this is The Morning Shift — your daily roundup of the top automotive headlines from around the world, in one place. This is where you'll find the most important stories that are shaping the way Americans drive and get around.
In this morning's edition we're looking at the Department of Defense getting GM and Ford into the drone business, and Tesla pushing its driver assistance on the elderly. We'll also look at Volkswagen's battle against its workers, and Ineos moving stateside. 
If you'd like a recap of the latest auto news sent to your inbox each weekday, you can sign up for Jalopnik's free Morning Shift newsletter here.
Under the Trump administration, the United States government has shifted from a focus on soft power — international aid, alliances, things of that nature — to focusing entirely on hard military power. Gone are the days of USAID, now is the era of massing drones. Only, there's a problem: U.S. drone makers can't actually make enough to keep up with Department of Defense orders. Thus, a new push from the Pentagon, to get automakers into the drone business. From the Wall Street Journal: 
The Pentagon is in talks with General Motors and other manufacturers to help mass produce drones, according to people familiar with the matter, the latest effort by the U.S. military to enlist the car industry's sprawling assembly lines to increase production of critical munitions and equipment.
...
Under a deal, auto companies would supply parts to drone companies, the people said.
Among the scenarios: GM is weighing whether to manufacture drone motors, albeit closer in size to something that would power a riding lawn mower than a car. Defense officials have met with leaders at Ford Motor, Canadian parts giant Magna International and other manufacturing companies, including outside the auto industry.
Responses from automakers around drone building have been ""mixed,"" according to the WSJ, but we live in an era where the President can hand-pick who runs companies in spite of all manner of allegations. Do we really think any U.S.-based company in its right mind would say no to such a man? Maybe an automaker could stall until the new legislative session in January, but that doesn't remove Trump from power. 
Tesla doesn't so much care about selling its cars as it does selling its software these days, and the company seems to have its sights set on a new frontier of driver-assistance software: The elderly. If you're wondering what happens when you combine technology that lulls drivers into a false sense of security with drivers whose reflexes and powers of perception are failing them, well, we're about to find out. From Bloomberg: 
Tesla warns consumers that FSD requires drivers to pay attention and be prepared to take immediate action at all times. It acknowledges none of its driver-assistance features make Teslas autonomous. And yet the company is increasingly promoting the merits of the system to seniors, further muddling the already complex question of when the moment has come for older drivers to give up their car keys.
Experts say the technology's limitations may actually raise risks for the elderly. The pitfalls of aging, including changes in vision, loss of physical mobility and slower reflexes, can impair an older driver's ability to safely take control when needed.
The core issue here is a thorny one. On the one hand, seniors still need to get around — grocery delivery isn't always an option on a fixed income, and socializing staves off dementia. On the other hand, people whose faculties are decaying probably shouldn't be controlling thousands of pounds of metal that hurtle through our roads, especially with software that only demands occasional-yet-immediate engagement rather than constant attention. The answer is buses, but no one wants to talk about that. 
Volkswagen, one of the world's largest carmakers, thinks it's too big. To compete with lower-cost competitors from China, the company is trying to scale back its own costs — by cutting production and people. Those people are, understandably, not super psyched about these decisions. From Reuters: 
EMDEN, Germany, Nov 24 (Reuters) – Volkswagen began crunch ‌wage talks with labour representatives on Tuesday amid mounting worker anger over deep cost cuts, with protesters carrying black coffins to symbolise what they said was the death of trust and the company's future prospects.
...
The first round of talks takes place in the northern German ​town of Emden, home to one of the factories fighting for survival, where around 4,000 workers protested before representatives of the IG Metall ​union ad the automaker's works council met Volkswagen management.
""If necessary, there will also be industrial action of a kind never before seen at Volkswagen,"" works council head Daniela Cavallo told workers chanting ""strike-ready"" and carrying black coffins inscribed with the words ""future"" and ""trust"".
When your workers start carrying coffins around to show the long-term costs of your short-sighted decision making, it's probably worth listening to them. Volkswagen's idea to cut its staff and production capacity to the bone is the sort of move that works on a spreadsheet, but realistically hampers the company's ability to be agile in a market that's seen constant changes from shifting international regulations and new competitors. Essentially, the company is looking to specialize in doing things in a way that works right now, with the hope that the current circumstances never change. 
Ineos, a company I occasionally remember exists and is real, wants to sell in the U.S. without tariffs. To do so, it's looking to start production on our shores, and it's going to make the numbers work by getting rid of one of its European factories — specifically, by selling a French factory to BYD. From Automotive News: 
Ineos Automotive, hampered by President Donald Trump's import tariffs, is ready to build in the U.S.
The small British automaker said it has identified a short list of U.S. sites where it plans to produce pickups and SUVs. At the same time the company, in a major pivot, plans to sell its French assembly plant to Chinese automaker BYD.
The two moves would have major repercussions on both sides of the Atlantic Ocean: Ineos would gain a manufacturing footprint in the country where it generates 70 percent of its global sales, and BYD would collect another European plant.
This is the kind of knock-on effect that's hard to predict when you institute tariffs. Sure, production of the Ineos Grenadier expands into the U.S., and that plays well with the UAW folks from whom Trump wants votes, it also enables a further BYD foothold in Europe, though, emboldening a Chinese competitor that Trump would rather see quashed. 


This bastion of legalized slavery is for women!
God only knows what the hell is happening with the economy now. Gas prices were skyrocketing, and now nothing's all that different but the prices are plummeting. Stocks were bad when Jensen Huang had affairs, but now he's back in the job that enabled all the affairs and the stocks are good, but later today they're set to be bad again. Anyone who says they can predict anything is a liar.

The combination of StarHub, M1 and Circles.Life would put it close to market leader Singtel in subscriber numbers.
StarHub and M1 logos seen at Northpoint City shopping mall and Causeway Point shopping mall on Nov 24, 2026. (Photos: CNA/Ili Mansor)

       This audio is generated by an AI tool.
    
SINGAPORE: A proposed StarHub-M1 merger would not affect mobile plan prices by the two telcos and their sub-brands before the transaction is expected to close in the first half of 2027, StarHub executives said on Tuesday (Nov 24).
StarHub on Tuesday announced its proposed consolidation with M1, as well as the acquisition of Circles.Life Singapore.
The StarHub-M1 merger, which is subject to regulatory and shareholder approval, is expected to close by mid-2027 alongside the Circles.Life acquisition.
It would bring mobile virtual network operators (MVNOs) Giga, Eight and MyRepublic and M1's Maxx under the same umbrella as Circles.Life.

This would be a ""powerful brand portfolio"" that ranges across offerings, StarHub CEO Nikhil Eapen told reporters.StarHub CEO-designate Matt Williams said the intention is not to make changes to pricing in 2027 as the company is focused on ensuring that customers stay with these brands, and that the businesses grow together.""At the moment, each of those brands is successfully addressing different groups of customers. And in each of those customer groups … we're also seeing increasingly strong performance,"" he said.He added: ""Our intention is not to make changes during 2027 because our focus during that year is really on making sure that we bring the businesses together, we keep all the customers happy.""Asked if the MVNOs will remain as such or be consolidated, Mr Williams said each of the sub-brands resonates strongly with their respective target customer segment.""We have separate teams looking after our MyRepublic business, looking after our Eight business, looking after our Giga business, so we see that continuing.""These teams who are really focused on those customers know the propositions that we have in each of those segments really well, and so they will continue to operate in that way,"" he added.There are currently no plans for headcount changes, although this is something that the company will look at ""further out"" given that a merger is involved, said Mr Williams.Any changes would be carefully made and in accordance with the relevant tripartite guidelines, he added.The combined headcount of StarHub, M1 and Circles.Life is about 2,700 employees.IMPACT ON PRICES AND MARKETThe merger would turn Singapore's telco market into a race between three major operators – the proposed StarHub-M1 entity, Singtel and Simba.Analysts previously said that less competition could ease pricing pressures on telcos and reduce customer perks.Tech industry observer Oo Gin Lee expected significant changes for mobile customers as the combination of StarHub, M1 and Circles.Life would put it close to market leader Singtel in subscriber numbers.StarHub and M1 have a combined customer base of 4.1 million, according to their statement on the proposed merger. the past few years,"" he said.Mr Oo said that even if the merged M1-StarHub entity does not increase prices in 2027, it will look for ways to increase its profits per user by cutting costs or locking said.""That means Simba can still compete at the S$10 price point, and any attempt by Singtel and StarHub to increase prices will have to be balanced against Simba's offering.""If not for Simba, it is also likely that StarHub and M1 would combine their sub-brands, as that would lead to cost savings and improve earnings, he added.""As long as there are S$10 plans, the telcos cannot ignore this and
