them could lead to regulatory issues, since the Infocomm Media Development Authority (IMDA) must be satisfied that the merger will not lead to anti-competitive behaviour, he said.Simba, previously known as TPG Telecom, was Singapore's fourth telco when it launched in 2017.Mr Oo said the impact of the proposed merger on Simba is hard to predict because IMDA is still investigating the company and has not decided on any penalties yet.Simba's parent company Tuas said on Nov 17 that it does not contest several regulatory breaches, including the unauthorised use of spectrum bands and 5G equipment. It faces possible action from IMDA.
This would be a ""powerful brand portfolio"" that ranges across offerings, StarHub CEO Nikhil Eapen told reporters.
StarHub CEO-designate Matt Williams said the intention is not to make changes to pricing in 2027 as the company is focused on ensuring that customers stay with these brands, and that the businesses grow together.
""At the moment, each of those brands is successfully addressing different groups of customers. And in each of those customer groups … we're also seeing increasingly strong performance,"" he said.
He added: ""Our intention is not to make changes during 2027 because our focus during that year is really on making sure that we bring the businesses together, we keep all the customers happy.""
Asked if the MVNOs will remain as such or be consolidated, Mr Williams said each of the sub-brands resonates strongly with their respective target customer segment.
""We have separate teams looking after our MyRepublic business, looking after our Eight business, looking after our Giga business, so we see that continuing.
""These teams who are really focused on those customers know the propositions that we have in each of those segments really well, and so they will continue to operate in that way,"" he added.
There are currently no plans for headcount changes, although this is something that the company will look at ""further out"" given that a merger is involved, said Mr Williams.
Any changes would be carefully made and in accordance with the relevant tripartite guidelines, he added.
The combined headcount of StarHub, M1 and Circles.Life is about 2,700 employees.
The merger would turn Singapore's telco market into a race between three major operators – the proposed StarHub-M1 entity, Singtel and Simba.
Analysts previously said that less competition could ease pricing pressures on telcos and reduce customer perks.
Tech industry observer Oo Gin Lee expected significant changes for mobile customers as the combination of StarHub, M1 and Circles.Life would put it close to market leader Singtel in subscriber numbers.
StarHub and M1 have a combined customer base of 4.1 million, according to their statement on the proposed merger. StarHub estimated Circles.Life's subscriber base at 290,000.
Singtel has 4.5 million mobile customers, according to its FY2026 report. Simba in 2025 said it had reached 1 million subscribers by early 2024 in its regulatory submission to IMDA.
The proposed StarHub-M1 merger involves two mobile network operators ""struggling with thin profit margins"" that have to find a way to increase earnings, said Mr Oo.
Mr Eapen told reporters StarHub and M1 would achieve a scale ""that we have never had before"" across financial, business, valuation and network infrastructure domains through the merger.
""Each of us has a significant cost base that is frankly disproportionate to our profitability and value, given the market erosion over the past few years,"" he said.
Mr Oo said that even if the merged M1-StarHub entity does not increase prices in 2027, it will look for ways to increase its profits per user by cutting costs or locking users into longer contracts.
The changes may not come as an outright price hike, said Mr Puar Leong Sing, Nanyang Polytechnic's course manager for the diploma in business management.
However, he said, they could take the form of ""fewer promotions or less generous data packages, gradually reducing consumer surplus in ways that are harder to detect"".
Nevertheless, there could be some benefits for customers, he noted: ""Sharing networks and infrastructure could lower costs and lead to better 5G coverage and improved reliability, and some of these efficiency gains may be passed on to consumers.""
But much depends on what Simba does, he added.
Simba has remained profitable at under S$10 average revenue per user (ARPU), while the other telcos reach about S$20 ARPU, he said.
""That means Simba can still compete at the S$10 price point, and any attempt by Singtel and StarHub to increase prices will have to be balanced against Simba's offering.""
If not for Simba, it is also likely that StarHub and M1 would combine their sub-brands, as that would lead to cost savings and improve earnings, he added.
""As long as there are S$10 plans, the telcos cannot ignore this and need to have at least two different brands to compete versus Simba while trying to focus on (their) higher value customers.""
They may also have chosen to retain multiple sub-brands because combining them could lead to regulatory issues, since the Infocomm Media Development Authority (IMDA) must be satisfied that the merger will not lead to anti-competitive behaviour, he said.
Simba, previously known as TPG Telecom, was Singapore's fourth telco when it launched in 2017.
Mr Oo said the impact of the proposed merger on Simba is hard to predict because IMDA is still investigating the company and has not decided on any penalties yet.
Simba's parent company Tuas said on Nov 17 that it does not contest several regulatory breaches, including the unauthorised use of spectrum bands and 5G equipment. It faces possible action from IMDA.
Mr Oo said Simba commands a unique position among MVNOs because it has invested in building its own network infrastructure, unlike other MVNOs that lease network capacity from the major telcos.
This means that while major telcos can block other MVNOs' access to their network capacity, the same is not true for Simba and ""you can't just remove Simba"", he said.
He added that it was unlikely Simba's licence would be removed by the regulator as doing so would further whittle the number of major network operators in the market down to two.
Dr Xu Le, lecturer at the National University of Singapore Business School, said the merged entity would be in a stronger position to compete with Singtel and Simba.
""On the one hand, the merger between StarHub and M1 expands the scale and scope of its business, allowing it to serve a wider range of customer segments.
""On the other hand, the acquisition of Circles.Life, with its asset-light and digital-first business model, could help the combined entity achieve greater operating efficiency and compete more effectively in the price-sensitive market segment,"" she said.
She added that whether pricing plans change after 2027 depends on the merged entity's operational efficiency and how intense market competition is.
""Competitors such as Simba and Singtel are unlikely to remain passive. They will adjust their own strategies to protect their market share and profitability,"" she said, pointing to their response as crucial in shaping the telco pricing landscape going forward.
On retaining the sub-brands, Dr Xu said this gives StarHub greater flexibility to charge different prices and cater to the preferences and willingness to pay of different customer segments.
""However, this strategy may also lead to cannibalisation, where an increase in sales of one brand comes at the expense of another brand within the same company,"" she said.
""In addition, if the brands are not sufficiently differentiated, it may create confusion among customers and lead to brand dilution.""
Dr Xu said that looking ahead, further consolidation was more likely to occur among smaller market players, particularly MVNOs.
While the liberalisation of Singapore's telco market, which began in 2000, has increased competition and made services more affordable, the industry is still characterised by high upfront investment and substantial fixed costs, she added.
""Given Singapore’s relatively small market, having more competitors means that each operator may have a smaller customer base over which to spread its fixed costs, resulting in higher average costs and lower profitability,"" she said.
""This creates an incentive for operators to pursue consolidation to achieve greater economies of scale, improve profitability and reduce the pressure of cut-throat price competition.""
Mr Puar said supporting several separate 5G networks in a relatively small market like Singapore is costly and arguably inefficient. He agreed that future consolidation may focus on smaller MVNOs.
""The regulatory challenge will be the need to balance the benefits of competition with the realities of long-term network efficiency,"" he said.
Mr Eapen said on Tuesday that it would put in its regulatory submissions “as soon as tomorrow” and it is expecting the launch of the public consultation “quite soon”.
IMDA said in response to CNA’s queries that it would assess the StarHub-M1 application in accordance with the Telecom and Media Competition Code, but consolidations involving MVNOs do not require its approval.
Mr Eapen said the company has had ""good engagement"" with regulators on the proposed transaction, but stopped short of commenting on expected hurdles when asked.
""We are receptive to the focus and the objectives of all stakeholders, and we anticipate and hope for an efficient and productive regulatory process,"" he added.
He also said some lessons were learnt from a previous attempted acquisition of M1 – a reference to an earlier unsuccessful deal by rival Simba, owned by Australia's Tuas.
Dr Xu said the failure of Simba's proposed acquisition of M1 did not necessarily suggest IMDA is opposed to consolidation among telcos.
""The key consideration for regulators is whether the benefits of consolidation will come at the expense of consumers' interests,” she added.
""They will need to assess the degree of market concentration and the overall market structure following the merger, as well as whether sufficient competition will remain.
""They will also need to evaluate whether the combined entity can achieve significant cost savings through improved operational efficiency and, more importantly, whether these cost savings will be passed on to consumers.”
Mr Puar said regulators could scrutinise spectrum concentration and seek to guarantee fair wholesale access for the remaining operators.
They could also seek to formalise StarHub's 2027 price hold as a ""binding commitment"", he added.

Finally some good news for SSD shoppers

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

After more than a year in which practically every memory headline has pointed upward, the flash market has finally blinked.
New reports have claimed NAND spot prices have slid roughly 20% in a matter of weeks as Chinese traders rush to clear inventory, with analysts now penciling in contract price cuts for early 2027.
Not long ago, the conversation was about shortages lasting several more years, but there is a catch attached to the good news: NAND is turning, but DRAM, which is also a key component for SSDs, is only wobbling at best for now
The sharpest numbers come from a Bank of America note reported by the Seoul Economic Daily which puts the spot price of a 512Gb NAND wafer at $14.30, down 12% in a week and 32% in the previous quarter, while 1Tb parts have dropped 19% to $24.90.
The bank says spot NAND has corrected by around 20% since mid-to-late October 2026, and it now expects general-purpose NAND, the kind found in consumer SSDs, laptops and phones, to fall a further 5% to 10% on contract in the first quarter of 2027.
TrendForce has arrived at much the same place, forecasting overall NAND contract prices to slip 0% to 5% quarter over quarter in Q1 2027, with every product category entering a downward cycle from Q2 and ""quarterly price drops potentially widening as the year progresses."" It has also cut its forecast for this quarter's contract increase to 8% to 13%, down from the 15% to 20% it projected at the end of September. Contract prices, in other words, are still rising today. The decline is a 2027 story.
Supply and demand are moving against sellers at the same time: TrendForce expects NAND bit supply to grow 25% to 30% in 2027, backed by industry capital spending of $29.9 billion, up 26.7%, as QLC adoption and higher layer counts squeeze more bits out of every wafer.
Sign up to the TechRadar Pro newsletter to get all the top news, opinion, features and guidance your business needs to succeed!
 



On the demand side, Chinese cloud providers have cut their 2027 enterprise SSD orders by more than US hyperscalers have added, citing GPU shortages, elevated inventory, and fierce competition on AI token pricing.
Consumers have played their part too, mostly by not buying: PC sales have been falling off a cliff in 2026, and phone and laptop makers trying to contain costs have been trimming storage specs, which shrinks NAND demand even when unit sales hold up.
One could say that the market structure explains why NAND was the first to crack: NAND has six major suppliers, against the three that control most of the DRAM market, and roughly 20% to 30% of global NAND capacity sits on older lines that are poorly suited to enterprise products and keep churning out commodity flash regardless.
One industry official told the Seoul Economic Daily that the weakness ""is being led by some commodity NAND used in smartphones and PCs,"" while demand for enterprise NAND in AI data centers ""remains strong.""
The move might not be properly visible in retail channels yet, and with good reason: Spot prices are the fast-moving fringe of the market, and they cover only a small slice of NAND volume. Most flash is sold on contract, and contract prices trail spot by weeks or months, and retail trails further still, especially while distributors work through stock bought near the peak.
If NAND is the good news, DRAM is the reason to keep the celebrations short. In TrendForce's week to November 17 2026, the mainstream DDR4 chip slipped 1.43% to $44.25 on the spot market, while branded DDR5 was ""mostly stable."" Even that small dip flatters the picture: DDR4 spot had been trading above suppliers' official quotes earlier in the month, so part of the drift is spot pricing settling back toward contract levels rather than a market in retreat.
On contract, DRAM is still climbing. Bank of America has kept its forecast of an 8% rise in Q4 2026 and another 5% in Q1 2027. TrendForce's latest DRAM bulletin says contract prices will keep rising, albeit with gains that shrink each quarter, while mobile DRAM negotiations have stalled as brands sit on their hands. Slower increases are the closest thing to relief on offer.
The reason, once again, is AI: ""HBM consumes three to four times more wafer resources than standard commodity DRAM,"" TrendForce researcher Ellie Wang said at the firm's Seoul roadshow on November 18. ""That is another reason we do not expect DRAM prices to turn lower in 2027,"" Wang said, adding that new capacity will only begin entering the market from late 2027, with Samsung's Pyeongtaek P5 and SK hynix's Yongin cluster coming online between 2028 and 2030.
The rest of the industry is still talking like it's a seller's market. Micron CEO Sanjay Mehrotra recently said that ""75% of our 2027 [supply] is sold out,"" SK hynix has warned that the shortage could persist through 2030, and Intel's chief has reportedly said we have another two to three years of this. Apple CEO John Ternus, meanwhile, described current memory pricing as ""unprecedented"". DRAM revenue rose 369% year over year in Q3, while bit shipments rose only around 4% in the quarter, suggesting the market is pricing scarcity, not volume.
For NAND, however, TrendForce lists several factors that could change the picture: how quickly Chinese cloud providers burn through their inventory, any shift in restrictions on overseas GPU procurement, and whether device makers keep shipping lower-storage specs into 2028. It also flags US policy after the midterm elections, particularly for AI data center projects planned for 2028 that are still in planning or permitting, since approvals there would drive advance buying in the second half of 2027.
Sellers have levers of their own. Some suppliers may slow expansion in response to weak consumer demand, according to TrendForce, and any cut to consumer flash wafer starts, or a wave of restocking by phone makers keen to buy low, could turn this dip into a brief one. Higher-end storage often incorporates DRAM, especially in enterprise-grade products, and it continues to command an increasing share of the total cost of storage currently being procured by hyperscalers.
Despite this, this is, without much doubt, the most encouraging memory and storage news in a long while, and it comes from more than one direction. A bank, a research firm, and the spot market itself all point the same way for NAND. If you are planning a storage upgrade and can afford to wait, patience into 2027 looks more likely to be rewarded than punished.
But the memory crisis has split in two rather than ended. Enterprise SSD pricing is still being propped up by US AI demand, and consumer flash is getting cheaper largely because Chinese cloud firms and shoppers have stopped buying, not because supply has caught up with the AI boom. DRAM has no equivalent pressure valve while HBM keeps swallowing wafers, so the best RAM buyers can hope for in 2027 is smaller increases. Anyone who cannot wait is still better off hunting for discounts, and Black Friday remains your best shot at that.

Rahim Amir is a UAE-based tech writer who enjoys building PCs as much as he enjoys writing about them. He has been professionally writing about PC hardware since 2023, focusing on buyer’s guides, hardware reviews, and sponsored content and features related to tech.
Having built hundreds of gaming PCs and being an avid gamer in his spare time, Rahim tends to have stronger opinions about hardware than most. This is particularly on display when he gets his way with powerful, but minimalistic RGB builds even as Small Form Factor (SFF) PCs come a close second.

81,2026-11-24,"A Wealth of Common Sense
Posted November 24, 2026 by Ben Carlson
Nvidia’s stock price was down more than 30% heading into last week.
Then Jensen Huang resigned and all hell broke loose.
The stock was down nearly 23% last week alone. That left NVDA almost 50% off its highs which were reached in the first week of October.
That’s an enormous crash for one of the biggest, most important companies in the world.
Last week’s stock market swoon is likely the biggest reason the board reinstated Huang as CEO Monday morning.
The shareholders, employees and President of the United States thought Huang was so important to the company, they did everything in their power to bring him back:
There are a few lessons here that are worth considering.
The stock market usually gets what it wants. In the fall of 2008, the House voted down the TARP bailout package for the banks. The stock market threw a tantrum and fell close to 10% that day. The bailout passed a couple of days later.
In March of 2020, the stock market crashed 35% in a matter of weeks when Covid changed the world. The market falling apart is one of the big reasons the Fed and government acted so swiftly with fiscal and monetary policy to shore up the economy.
In the spring of last year following the Liberation Day tariff announcements, the stock market dropped 5% and 6% on back-to-back days. The S&P 500 was down 19% in the blink of an eye. This caused the President to put a 90-day pause on the tariffs.
I think you could make the case that everyone acted so quickly to bring Jensen back to Nvidia because the stock market sold off so hard last week. Nvidia getting cut in half forced everyone’s hand.
The stock market got what it wanted…again.
Jensen is THE key man in the AI buildout. On Friday Nvidia closed with a market cap of $3 trillion. After the stock rose more than 30% on Monday, the market cap was $4 trillion.
Is Jensen worth $1 trillion to the company? It’s possible.
Ben Thompson explained the importance of Huang to Nvidia in a piece last week at Stratechery:
Nvidia’s most important moat, however, was the degree to which it was not a normal company. Huang organized Nvidia as an exoskeleton around one man: himself. There is very little management at Nvidia — Huang famously has 60 direct reports — for the same reason that there was no succession plan: Nvidia could move so quickly and so aggressively because the company still behaved as if it were a startup. Indeed, lots of established companies — including those still led by founders — claim to still be a startup, but only one, Nvidia, really was organized that way. That paid off with all of those successful bets; it also created an environment where there were no checks on Huang. And, now that he is gone, the exoskeleton is much more poised to collapse than you might expect.
I knew this was a powerful founder, but I may have been naive about just how powerful he really is for this multi-trillion-dollar company.
The tech industry likes it when a founder wields all of the power. That’s also an enormous key person risk for a technology that costs trillions of dollars to build.
The rich and powerful seem to operate with their own set of rules. A lot of people will be rightfully upset by how this all went down.
Obviously, the board fumbled this process from the outset. They could have completed their investigation before forcing his hand to resign. Plus, on last week’s earnings call they explicitly said Jensen would not return as CEO. There are going to be some lawsuits here:
In many ways, it feels like the rich and powerful are immune to the consequences of their actions. It’s always been like this to some degree, but it feels like it’s more apparent now than ever before.
In her novel, The Glass Hotel, Emily St. John Mandel writes about a character who starts a relationship with an uber-rich Bernie Madoff-like hedge fund huckster.
After living a life of luxury, she comes to a realization about what it’s like to be fabulously wealthy:
You know what I’ve learned about money? I was trying to figure out why my life felt more or less the same in Singapore as it did in London, and that’s when I realized that money is its own country.
The stock market feels like it is its own country too.
The stock market is amoral. The stock market has no morals or ethics. It does not have political affiliations. The stock market has no feelings.
It only cares about profits and growth.
Sometimes things can happen with companies or the market that you don’t personally agree with.
You don’t have to like it but it’s important to understand this dynamic.
The stock market is more important than ever. I wrote an entire chapter in Risk & Reward about the history of stock market ownership in America because that trend is one of the most important forces of the economy in the past 50 years.
Now that two-thirds of households own stocks in some form, the incentives are much different than they were in the past when 10-20% of households owned stocks.
The stock market is more powerful than ever. It’s a driver of economic activity. It’s how people save and invest for retirement.
Everyone wants it to go up. You don’t have to like it, you don’t have to agree with it but in some ways the stock market is now too big to fail.
Are we adding new and unintended risks to the equation by focusing so much attention on the stock market?
Absolutely.
I just don’t know when or how those risks will rear their ugly head.
When we do have a financial crisis that impacts the stock market in a big way, the pain is going to be felt more broadly than it was in the past.

      Steve Inskeep
    

How will a Democratic-majority Congress view President Trump's involvement in business and the stock market? NPR's Steve Inskeep asks Democratic Rep. Raja Krishnamoorthi of Illinois.
STEVE INSKEEP, HOST:  Now let's hear one of the lawmakers who expects to question Jensen Huang next week. Representative Raja Krishnamoorthi is a Democrat from Illinois. Welcome back.RAJA KRISHNAMOORTHI: Hey. Thank you, Steve.INSKEEP: OK. So Nvidia received an assurance from the U.S. government, they say, that Jensen Huang's return poses no national security risk. What do you make of that?KRISHNAMOORTHI: I think that reassurance rings hollow to me. Obviously, the president received a $25 million donation from Nvidia to his ballroom. He personally has traded on Nvidia stocks, so he stands to gain from Nvidia shares going up. But, you know, the FBI hasn't concluded its investigation into Huang and Nvidia, and Nvidia itself hasn't concluded its own investigation. So his reinstatement, to me, seems outrageous and reckless.INSKEEP: I'm just trying to work through the logic here and make the strongest possible case I could for the other side. Could you say that Jensen Huang perhaps unknowingly had an affair with this person, but the affair seems likely over now, and he'll go on and do his business?KRISHNAMOORTHI: Well, we don't know the answers to these questions. We don't know what Alice Huang learned from him and passed along to Chinese Communist Party spy masters. She had been meeting with them for a year, overlapping with her affair with Huang - Jensen Huang. And we don't know what Jensen Huang disclosed to Alice Huang in terms of nonpublic information, either about Nvidia's trade secrets or about national security secrets. Remember, there are members of Nvidia - employees - who are granted top-secret classified clearance and have access to our national security secrets. And we don't know if Alice Huang gained access to any of those.INSKEEP: I want to read a long quote here from the president. This is from the CNBC interview that Danielle mentioned. And he tried to make the argument that it would be bad for national security not to let Jensen Huang back. And I'm just going to read the president's words. These are the president's words. Quote, ""I could see China - if we didn't do this, I could see China coming in and saying to Jensen, hey. How would you like to come to China? He's got a little blood in him, you know? He's got a little blood there."" Again, these are the president's words. ""How would you like to come to China and head up Huawei? OK. Head up one of our companies. And, you know, if Jensen was angry enough, if he was treated badly, maybe he could do that.""How would you respond to that argument by the president of the United States?KRISHNAMOORTHI: We don't have any information or basis to believe that Huang was approached by the Chinese in this way. And I think that it just feels slightly racist to me, the way he said that. In any case - look. I think there are too many questions unanswered here about the investigation into Nvidia. Remember, the same day that Jensen Huang was reinstated, Megaspeed, which is the company controlled by Alice Huang, his...INSKEEP: Yeah.KRISHNAMOORTHI: ...Mistress, was blacklisted, along with numerous other affiliates. Why? Because essentially, there's evidence that Alice Huang and Megaspeed smuggled these high-end semiconductor chips to China or gave remote access to these chips to blacklisted entities. And so to say that, you know, all of our national security concerns have been addressed is deeply - a deeply flawed assessment. And I think now Congress has to launch an independent investigation and pass export control laws that are much stronger than the ones we have now, including my legislation, the Chip Security Act, which is before the Senate, to remove the discretion that the Trump administration otherwise has in executing our export control laws.INSKEEP: When you say just a little bit racist there, I want to make sure that we're clear what you're saying. Trump said this thing about how he's got a little blood there...KRISHNAMOORTHI: Yeah.INSKEEP: ...Seemingly meaning a little Chinese blood, and therefore, he wouldn't - might not be loyal. That's the part that sounds racist to you. Am I right?KRISHNAMOORTHI: Correct. It's the dual-loyalty trope.INSKEEP: OK. How concerned are you about the economic effects of all this? Clearly, the stock market liked Jensen Huang in, did not like Jensen Huang out. And it's clear that in addition to his view of selling chips to China, he had a particular view of financing the entire AI boom. What do you make of that side of this?KRISHNAMOORTHI: Well, look. I understand that Trump wanted to see Nvidia shares go up. We don't know, by the way, what trades he made yesterday before the announcement was made to reinstate Huang. However, the question is, is our national security for sale? Why do we have - your listeners may be wondering - why do we care about this? It's because these high-end semiconductor chips in the hands of our adversaries, especially the Chinese Communist Party, get deployed into their military modernization program at our expense. We would never, ever export nuclear submarines or aircraft carriers to the Chinese Communist Party. Why would we do that with the highest-end semiconductor chips? And that's what's at issue right now.INSKEEP: It seems to me there is some - at least a little bit of merit to Jensen Huang's own argument that when we deny high-end chips to China, it incentivizes China to try to develop its own high-end chips. Do you think that is happening?KRISHNAMOORTHI: They're already doing it. They - Huawei and other chipmakers in China are already trying to wean themselves off dependence on Nvidia chips. However, they haven't been able to gain access to the secret sauce of either the design or the manufacture of those chips, by the way, by TSMC, a large corporation within Taiwan. And so it's a fallacy to think that somehow the Chinese are going to try to remain independent or, sorry, dependent on us because they're trying to wean themselves off these chips to begin with. They're trying to steal IP. They're trying to do everything they can to basically develop their own capabilities here.INSKEEP: One other aspect of this to get into in the moment or so we have left - how significant is it that the president of the United States in some way intervened here? And to be honest, it doesn't seem like he's the only person who intervened. A lot of investors seem to have opinions. A lot of tech lords seem to have opinions. But the president said, I was involved. I was directly and indirectly involved.KRISHNAMOORTHI: Look, I think this is another example of kind of billionaire capture of our administration and the president using his powers to benefit himself. Another good example is he allowed chips to go to UAE recently. And the UAE basically had one of their sheikhs invest $500 million in a Trump family corporation, World...INSKEEP: OK.KRISHNAMOORTHI: ...Liberty Financial. So this is wrong. It's corrupt.INSKEEP: Much to investigate, and Raja Krishnamoorthi is one of the lawmakers who will be doing that - Democrat from Illinois. Thanks so much.KRISHNAMOORTHI: Thank you.(SOUNDBITE OF SURPRISE CHEF'S ""GET WELL SOON"")

Quote, ""I could see China - if we didn't do this, I could see China coming in and saying to Jensen, hey. How would you like to come to China? He's got a little blood in him, you know? He's got a little blood there."" Again, these are the president's words. ""How would you like to come to China and head up Huawei? OK. Head up one of our companies. And, you know, if Jensen was angry enough, if he was treated badly, maybe he could do that.""How would you respond to that argument by the president of the United States?KRISHNAMOORTHI: We don't have any information or basis to believe that Huang was approached by the Chinese in this way. And I think that it just feels slightly racist to me, the way he said that. In any case - look. I think there are too many questions unanswered here about the investigation into Nvidia. Remember, the same day that Jensen Huang was reinstated, Megaspeed, which is the company controlled by Alice Huang, his...INSKEEP: Yeah.KRISHNAMOORTHI: ...Mistress, was blacklisted, along with numerous other affiliates. Why? Because essentially, there's evidence that Alice Huang and Megaspeed smuggled these high-end semiconductor chips to China or gave remote access to these chips to blacklisted entities. And so to say that, you know, all of our national security concerns have been addressed is deeply - a deeply flawed assessment. And I think now Congress has to launch an independent investigation and pass export control laws that are much stronger than the ones we have now, including my legislation, the Chip Security Act, which is before the Senate, to remove the discretion that the Trump administration otherwise has in executing our export control laws.INSKEEP: When you say just a little bit racist there, I want to make sure that we're clear what you're saying. Trump said this thing about how he's got a little blood there...KRISHNAMOORTHI: Yeah.INSKEEP: ...Seemingly meaning a little Chinese blood, and therefore, he wouldn't - might not be loyal. That's the part that sounds racist to you. Am I right?KRISHNAMOORTHI: Correct. It's the dual-loyalty trope.INSKEEP: OK. How concerned are you about the economic effects of all this? Clearly, the stock market liked Jensen Huang in, did not like Jensen Huang out. And it's clear that in addition to his view of selling chips to China, he had a particular view of financing the entire AI boom. What do you make of that side of this?KRISHNAMOORTHI: Well, look. I understand that Trump wanted to see Nvidia shares go up. We don't know, by the way, what trades he made yesterday before the announcement was made to reinstate Huang. However, the question is, is our national security for sale? Why do we have - your listeners may be wondering - why do we care about this? It's because these high-end semiconductor chips in the hands of our adversaries, especially the Chinese Communist Party, get deployed into their military modernization program at our expense. We would never, ever export nuclear submarines or aircraft carriers to the Chinese Communist Party. Why would we do that with the highest-end semiconductor chips? And that's what's at issue right now.INSKEEP: It seems to me there is some - at least a little bit of merit to Jensen Huang's own argument that when we deny high-end chips to China, it incentivizes China to try to develop its own high-end chips. Do you think that is happening?KRISHNAMOORTHI: They're already doing it. They - Huawei and other chipmakers in China are already trying to wean themselves off dependence on Nvidia chips. However, they haven't been able to gain access to the secret sauce of either the design or the manufacture of those chips, by the way, by TSMC, a large corporation within Taiwan. And so it's a fallacy to think that somehow the Chinese are going to try to remain independent or, sorry, dependent on us because they're trying to wean themselves off these chips to begin with. They're trying to steal IP. They're trying to do everything they can to basically develop their own capabilities here.INSKEEP: One other aspect of this to get into in the moment or so we have left - how significant is it that the president of the United States in some way intervened here? And to be honest, it doesn't seem like he's the only person who intervened. A lot of investors seem to have opinions. A lot of tech lords seem to have opinions. But the president said, I was involved. I was directly and indirectly involved.KRISHNAMOORTHI: Look, I think this is another example of kind of billionaire capture of our administration and the president using his powers to benefit himself. Another good example is he allowed chips to go to UAE recently. And the UAE basically had one of their sheikhs invest $500 million in a Trump family corporation, World...INSKEEP: OK.KRISHNAMOORTHI: ...Liberty Financial. So this is wrong. It's corrupt.INSKEEP: Much to investigate, and Raja Krishnamoorthi is one of the lawmakers who will be doing that - Democrat from Illinois. Thanks so much.KRISHNAMOORTHI: Thank you.(SOUNDBITE OF SURPRISE CHEF'S ""GET WELL SOON"")

RAJA KRISHNAMOORTHI: Hey. Thank you, Steve.INSKEEP: OK. So Nvidia received an assurance from the U.S. government, they say, that Jensen Huang's return poses no national security risk. What do you make of that?KRISHNAMOORTHI: I think that reassurance rings hollow to me. Obviously, the president received a $25 million donation from Nvidia to his ballroom. He personally has traded on Nvidia stocks, so he stands to gain from Nvidia shares going up. But, you know, the FBI hasn't concluded its investigation into Huang and Nvidia, and Nvidia itself hasn't concluded its own investigation. So his reinstatement, to me, seems outrageous and reckless.INSKEEP: I'm just trying to work through the logic here and make the strongest possible case I could for the other side. Could you say that Jensen Huang perhaps unknowingly had an affair with this person, but the affair seems likely over now, and he'll go on and do his business?KRISHNAMOORTHI: Well, we don't know the answers to these questions. We don't know what Alice Huang learned from him and passed along to Chinese Communist Party spy masters. She had been meeting with them for a year, overlapping with her affair with Huang - Jensen Huang. And we don't know what Jensen Huang disclosed to Alice Huang in terms of nonpublic information, either about Nvidia's trade secrets or about national security secrets. Remember, there are members of Nvidia - employees - who are granted top-secret classified clearance and have access to our national security secrets. And so it's a fallacy to think that somehow the Chinese are going to try to remain independent or, sorry, dependent on us because they're trying to wean themselves off these chips to begin with. They're trying to steal IP. They're trying to do everything they can to basically develop their own capabilities here.INSKEEP: One other aspect of this to get into in the moment or so we have left - how significant is it that the president of the United States in some way intervened here? And to be honest, it doesn't seem like he's the only person who intervened. A lot of investors seem to have opinions. A lot of tech lords seem to have opinions. But the president said, I was involved. I was directly and indirectly involved.KRISHNAMOORTHI: Look, I think this is another example of kind of billionaire capture of our administration and the president using his powers to benefit himself. Another good example is he allowed chips to go to UAE recently. And the UAE basically had one of their sheikhs invest $500 million in a Trump family corporation, World...INSKEEP: OK.KRISHNAMOORTHI: ...Liberty Financial. So this is wrong. It's corrupt.INSKEEP: Much to investigate, and Raja Krishnamoorthi is one of the lawmakers who will be doing that - Democrat from Illinois. Thanks so much.KRISHNAMOORTHI: Thank you.(SOUNDBITE OF SURPRISE CHEF'S ""GET WELL SOON"")

KRISHNAMOORTHI: I think that reassurance rings hollow to me. Obviously, the president received a $25 million donation from Nvidia to his ballroom. He personally has traded on Nvidia stocks, so he stands to gain from Nvidia shares going up. But, you know, the FBI hasn't concluded its investigation into Huang and Nvidia, and Nvidia itself hasn't concluded its own investigation. So his reinstatement, to me, seems outrageous and reckless.INSKEEP: I'm just trying to work through the logic here and make the strongest possible case I could for the other side. Could you say that Jensen Huang perhaps unknowingly had an affair with this person, but the affair seems likely over now, and he'll go on and do his business?KRISHNAMOORTHI: Well, we don't know the answers to these questions. We don't know what Alice Huang learned from him and passed along to Chinese Communist Party spy masters. She had been meeting with them for a year, overlapping with her affair with Huang - Jensen Huang. And we don't know what Jensen Huang disclosed to Alice Huang in terms of nonpublic information, either about Nvidia's trade secrets or about national security secrets. Remember, there are members of Nvidia - employees - who are granted top-secret classified clearance and have access to our national security secrets. And we don't know if Alice Huang gained access to any of those.INSKEEP: I want to read a long quote here from the president. This is from the CNBC interview that Danielle mentioned. And he tried to make the argument that it would be bad for national security not to let Jensen Huang back. And I'm just going to read the president's words. These are the president's words. Quote, ""I could see China - if we didn't do this, I could see China coming in and saying to Jensen, hey. How would you like to come to China? He's got a little blood in him, you know? He's got a little blood there."" Again, these are the president's words. ""How would you like to come to China and head up Huawei? OK. Head up one of our companies. And, you know, if Jensen was angry enough, if he was treated badly, maybe he could do that.""How would you respond to that argument by the president of the United States?KRISHNAMOORTHI: We don't have any information or basis to believe that Huang was approached by the Chinese in this way. And I think that it just feels slightly racist to me, the way he said that. In any case - look. I think there are too many questions unanswered here about the investigation into Nvidia. Remember, the same day that Jensen Huang was reinstated, Megaspeed, which is the company controlled by Alice Huang, his...INSKEEP: Yeah.KRISHNAMOORTHI: ...Mistress, was blacklisted, along with numerous other affiliates. Why? Because essentially, there's evidence that Alice Huang and Megaspeed smuggled these high-end semiconductor chips to China or gave remote access to these chips to blacklisted entities. And so to say that, you know, all of our national security concerns have been addressed is deeply - a deeply flawed assessment. And I think now Congress has to launch an independent investigation and pass export control laws that are much stronger than the ones we have now, including my legislation, the Chip Security Act, which is before the Senate, to remove the discretion that the Trump administration otherwise has in executing our export control laws.INSKEEP: When you say just a little bit racist there, I want to make sure that we're clear what you're saying. Trump said this thing about how he's got a little blood there...KRISHNAMOORTHI: Yeah.INSKEEP: ...Seemingly meaning a little Chinese blood, and therefore, he wouldn't - might not be loyal. That's the part that sounds racist to you. Am I right?KRISHNAMOORTHI: Correct. It's the dual-loyalty trope.INSKEEP: OK. How concerned are you about the economic effects of all this? Clearly, the stock market liked Jensen Huang in, did not like Jensen Huang out. And it's clear that in addition to his view of selling chips to China, he had a particular view of financing the entire AI boom. What do you make of that side of this?KRISHNAMOORTHI: Well, look. I understand that Trump wanted to see Nvidia shares go up. We don't know, by the way, what trades he made yesterday before the announcement was made to reinstate Huang. However, the question is, is our national security for sale? Why do we have - your listeners may be wondering - why do we care about this? It's because these high-end semiconductor chips in the hands of our adversaries, especially the Chinese Communist Party, get deployed into their military modernization program at our expense. We would never, ever export nuclear submarines or aircraft carriers to the Chinese Communist Party. Why would we do that with the highest-end semiconductor chips? And that's what's at issue right now.INSKEEP: It seems to me there is some - at least a little bit of merit to Jensen Huang's own argument that when we deny high-end chips to China, it incentivizes China to try to develop its own high-end chips. Do you think that is happening?KRISHNAMOORTHI: They're already doing it. They - Huawei and other chipmakers in China are already trying to wean themselves off dependence on Nvidia chips. However, they haven't been able to gain access to the secret sauce of either the design or the manufacture of those chips, by the way, by TSMC, a large corporation within Taiwan. And so it's a fallacy to think that somehow the Chinese are going to try to remain independent or, sorry, dependent on us because they're trying to wean themselves off these chips to begin with. They're trying to steal IP. They're trying to do everything they can to basically develop their own capabilities here.INSKEEP: One other aspect of this to get into in the moment or so we have left - how significant is it that the president of the United States in some way intervened here? And to be honest, it doesn't seem like he's the only person who intervened. A lot of investors seem to have opinions. A lot of tech lords seem to have opinions. But the president said, I was involved. I was directly and indirectly involved.KRISHNAMOORTHI: Look, I think this is another example of kind of billionaire capture of our administration and the president using his powers to benefit himself. Another good example is he allowed chips to go to UAE recently. And the UAE basically had one of their sheikhs invest $500 million in a Trump family corporation, World...INSKEEP: OK.KRISHNAMOORTHI: ...Liberty Financial. So this is wrong. And that's what's at issue right now.INSKEEP: It seems to me there is some - at least a little bit of merit to Jensen Huang's own argument that when we deny high-end chips to China, it incentivizes China to try to develop its own high-end chips. Do you think that is happening?KRISHNAMOORTHI: They're already doing it. They - Huawei and other chipmakers in China are already trying to wean themselves off dependence on Nvidia chips. However, they haven't been able to gain access to the secret sauce of either the design or the manufacture of those chips, by the way, by TSMC, a large corporation within Taiwan. And so it's a fallacy to think that somehow the Chinese are going to try to remain independent or, sorry, dependent on us because they're trying to wean themselves off these chips to begin with. They're trying to steal IP. They're trying to do everything they can to basically develop their own capabilities here.INSKEEP: One other aspect of this to get into in the moment or so we have left - how significant is it that the president of the United States in some way intervened here? And to be honest, it doesn't seem like he's the only person who intervened. A lot of investors seem to have opinions. A lot of tech lords seem to have opinions. But the president said, I was involved. I was directly and indirectly involved.KRISHNAMOORTHI: Look, I think this is another example of kind of billionaire capture of our administration and the president using his powers to benefit himself. Another good example is he allowed chips to go to UAE recently. And the UAE basically had one of their sheikhs invest $500 million in a Trump family corporation, World...INSKEEP: OK.KRISHNAMOORTHI: ...Liberty Financial. So this is wrong. It's corrupt.INSKEEP: Much to investigate, and Raja Krishnamoorthi is one of the lawmakers who will be doing that - Democrat from Illinois. Thanks so much.KRISHNAMOORTHI: Thank you.(SOUNDBITE OF SURPRISE CHEF'S ""GET WELL SOON"")

INSKEEP: I want to read a long quote here from the president. This is from the CNBC interview that Danielle mentioned. And he tried to make the argument that it would be bad for national security not to let Jensen Huang back. And I'm just going to read the president's words. These are the president's words. Quote, ""I could see China - if we didn't do this, I could see China coming in and saying to Jensen, hey. How would you like to come to China? He's got a little blood in him, you know? He's got a little blood there."" Again, these are the president's words. ""How would you like to come to China and head up Huawei? OK. Head up one of our companies. And, you know, if Jensen was angry enough, if he was treated badly, maybe he could do that.""How would you respond to that argument by the president of the United States?KRISHNAMOORTHI: We don't have any information or basis to believe that Huang was approached by the Chinese in this way. And I think that it just feels slightly racist to me, the way he said that. In any case - look. I think there are too many questions unanswered here about the investigation into Nvidia. Remember, the same day that Jensen Huang was reinstated, Megaspeed, which is the company controlled by Alice Huang, his...INSKEEP: Yeah.KRISHNAMOORTHI: ...Mistress, was blacklisted, along with numerous other affiliates. Why? Because essentially, there's evidence that Alice Huang and Megaspeed smuggled these high-end semiconductor chips to China or gave remote access to these chips to blacklisted entities. And so to say that, you know, all of our national security concerns have been addressed is deeply - a deeply flawed assessment. And I think now Congress has to launch an independent investigation and pass export control laws that are much stronger than the ones we have now, including my legislation, the Chip Security Act, which is before the Senate, to remove the discretion that the Trump administration otherwise has in executing our export control laws.INSKEEP: When you say just a little bit racist there, I want to make sure that we're clear what you're saying. Trump said this thing about how he's got a little blood there...KRISHNAMOORTHI: Yeah.INSKEEP: ...Seemingly meaning a little Chinese blood, and therefore, he wouldn't - might not be loyal. That's the part that sounds racist to you. Am I right?KRISHNAMOORTHI: Correct. It's the dual-loyalty trope.INSKEEP: OK. How concerned are you about the economic effects of all this? Clearly, the stock market liked Jensen Huang in, did not like Jensen Huang out. And it's clear that in addition to his view of selling chips to China, he had a particular view of financing the entire AI boom. What do you make of that side of this?KRISHNAMOORTHI: Well, look. I understand that Trump wanted to see Nvidia shares go up. We don't know, by the way, what trades he made yesterday before the announcement was made to reinstate Huang. However, the question is, is our national security for sale? Why do we have - your listeners may be wondering - why do we care about this? It's because these high-end semiconductor chips in the hands of our adversaries, especially the Chinese Communist Party, get deployed into their military modernization program at our expense. We would never, ever export nuclear submarines or aircraft carriers to the Chinese Communist Party. Why would we do that with the highest-end semiconductor chips? And that's what's at issue right now.INSKEEP: It seems to me there is some - at least a little bit of merit to Jensen Huang's own argument that when we deny high-end chips to China, it incentivizes China to try to develop its own high-end chips. Do you think that is happening?KRISHNAMOORTHI: They're already doing it. They - Huawei and other chipmakers in China are already trying to wean themselves off dependence on Nvidia chips. However, they haven't been able to gain access to the secret sauce of either the design or the manufacture of those chips, by the way, by TSMC, a large corporation within Taiwan. And so it's a fallacy to think that somehow the Chinese are going to try to remain independent or, sorry, dependent on us because they're trying to wean themselves off these chips to begin with. They're trying to steal IP. They're trying to do everything they can to basically develop their own capabilities here.INSKEEP: One other aspect of this to get into in the moment or so we have left - how significant is it that the president of the United States in some way intervened here? And to be honest, it doesn't seem like he's the only person who intervened. A lot of investors seem to have opinions. A lot of tech lords seem to have opinions. But the president said, I was involved. I was directly and indirectly involved.KRISHNAMOORTHI: Look, I think this is another example of kind of billionaire capture of our administration and the president using his powers to benefit himself. Another good example is he allowed chips to go to UAE recently. And the UAE basically had one of their sheikhs invest $500 million in a Trump family corporation, World...INSKEEP: OK.KRISHNAMOORTHI: ...Liberty Financial. So this is wrong. It's corrupt.INSKEEP: Much to investigate, and Raja Krishnamoorthi is one of the lawmakers who will be doing that - Democrat from Illinois. Thanks so much.KRISHNAMOORTHI: Thank you.(SOUNDBITE OF SURPRISE CHEF'S ""GET WELL SOON"")

And to be honest, it doesn't seem like he's the only person who intervened. A lot of investors seem to have opinions. A lot of tech lords seem to have opinions. But the president said, I was involved. I was directly and indirectly involved.KRISHNAMOORTHI: Look, I think this is another example of kind of billionaire capture of our administration and the president using his powers to benefit himself. Another good example is he allowed chips to go to UAE recently. And the UAE basically had one of their sheikhs invest $500 million in a Trump family corporation, World...INSKEEP: OK.KRISHNAMOORTHI: ...Liberty Financial. So this is wrong. It's corrupt.INSKEEP: Much to investigate, and Raja Krishnamoorthi is one of the lawmakers who will be doing that - Democrat from Illinois. Thanks so much.KRISHNAMOORTHI: Thank you.(SOUNDBITE OF SURPRISE CHEF'S ""GET WELL SOON"")

INSKEEP: Yeah.KRISHNAMOORTHI: ...Mistress, was blacklisted, along with numerous other affiliates. Why? Because essentially, there's evidence that Alice Huang and Megaspeed smuggled these high-end semiconductor chips to China or gave remote access to these chips to blacklisted entities. And so to say that, you know, all of our national security concerns have been addressed is deeply - a deeply flawed assessment. And I think now Congress has to launch an independent investigation and pass export control laws that are much stronger than the ones we have now, including my legislation, the Chip Security Act, which is before the Senate, to remove the discretion that the Trump administration otherwise has in executing our export control laws.INSKEEP: When you say just a little bit racist there, I want to make sure that we're clear what you're saying. Trump said this thing about how he's got a little blood there...KRISHNAMOORTHI: Yeah.INSKEEP: ...Seemingly meaning a little Chinese blood, and therefore, he wouldn't - might not be loyal. That's the part that sounds racist to you. Am I right?KRISHNAMOORTHI: Correct. It's the dual-loyalty trope.INSKEEP: OK. How concerned are you about the economic effects of all this? Clearly, the stock market liked Jensen Huang in, did not like Jensen Huang out. And it's clear that in addition to his view of selling chips to China, he had a particular view of financing the entire AI boom. What do you make of that side of this?KRISHNAMOORTHI: Well, look. I understand that Trump wanted to see Nvidia shares go up. We don't know, by the way, what trades he made yesterday before the announcement was made to reinstate Huang. However, the question is, is our national security for sale? Why do we have - your listeners may be wondering - why do we care about this? It's because these high-end semiconductor chips in the hands of our adversaries, especially the Chinese Communist Party, get deployed into their military modernization program at our expense. We would never, ever export nuclear submarines or aircraft carriers to the Chinese Communist Party. Why would we do that with the highest-end semiconductor chips? And that's what's at issue right now.INSKEEP: It seems to me there is some - at least a little bit of merit to Jensen Huang's own argument that when we deny high-end chips to China, it incentivizes China to try to develop its own high-end chips. Do you think that is happening?KRISHNAMOORTHI: They're already doing it. They - Huawei and other chipmakers in China are already trying to wean themselves off dependence on Nvidia chips. However, they haven't been able to gain access to the secret sauce of either the design or the manufacture of those chips, by the way, by TSMC, a large corporation within Taiwan. And so it's a fallacy to think that somehow the Chinese are going to try to remain independent or, sorry, dependent on us because they're trying to wean themselves off these chips to begin with. They're trying to steal IP. They're trying to do everything they can to basically develop their own capabilities here.INSKEEP: One other aspect of this to get into in the moment or so we have left - how significant is it that the president of the United States in some way intervened here? And to be honest, it doesn't seem like he's the only person who intervened. A lot of investors seem to have opinions. A lot of tech lords seem to have opinions. But the president said, I was involved. I was directly and indirectly involved.KRISHNAMOORTHI: Look, I think this is another example of kind of billionaire capture of our administration and the president using his powers to benefit himself. Another good example is he allowed chips to go to UAE recently. And the UAE basically had one of their sheikhs invest $500 million in a Trump family corporation, World...INSKEEP: OK.KRISHNAMOORTHI: ...Liberty Financial. So this is wrong. It's corrupt.INSKEEP: Much to investigate, and Raja Krishnamoorthi is one of the lawmakers who will be doing that - Democrat from Illinois. Thanks so much.KRISHNAMOORTHI: Thank you.(SOUNDBITE OF SURPRISE CHEF'S ""GET WELL SOON"")

And that's what's at issue right now.INSKEEP: It seems to me there is some - at least a little bit of merit to Jensen Huang's own argument that when we deny high-end chips to China, it incentivizes China to try to develop its own high-end chips. Do you think that is happening?KRISHNAMOORTHI: They're already doing it. They - Huawei and other chipmakers in China are already trying to wean themselves off dependence on Nvidia chips. However, they haven't been able to gain access to the secret sauce of either the design or the manufacture of those chips, by the way, by TSMC, a large corporation within Taiwan. And so it's a fallacy to think that somehow the Chinese are going to try to remain independent or, sorry, dependent on us because they're trying to wean themselves off these chips to begin with. They're trying to steal IP. They're trying to do everything they can to basically develop their own capabilities here.INSKEEP: One other aspect of this to get into in the moment or so we have left - how significant is it that the president of the United States in some way intervened here? And to be honest, it doesn't seem like he's the only person who intervened. A lot of investors seem to have opinions. A lot of tech lords seem to have opinions. But the president said, I was involved. I was directly and indirectly involved.KRISHNAMOORTHI: Look, I think this is another example of kind of billionaire capture of our administration and the president using his powers to benefit himself. Another good example is he allowed chips to go to UAE recently. And the UAE basically had one of their sheikhs invest $500 million in a Trump family corporation, World...INSKEEP: OK.KRISHNAMOORTHI: ...Liberty Financial. So this is wrong. It's corrupt.INSKEEP: Much to investigate, and Raja Krishnamoorthi is one of the lawmakers who will be doing that - Democrat from Illinois. Thanks so much.KRISHNAMOORTHI: Thank you.(SOUNDBITE OF SURPRISE CHEF'S ""GET WELL SOON"")

84,2026-11-24,"Author of the First Trade newsletter
When markets get crushed like they did last week — partially due to instability at Nvidia, the beating heart of the AI trade — snapbacks often materialize.
Dip-buyers were already out in force in Monday premarket trading. Then Nvidia and President Donald Trump poured a huge bucket of gasoline on the fire, and stocks were off to the races.
The chipmaker reinstated CEO Jensen Huang — after intervention from Trump — pushing the overall market to its best day in nearly 20 months. Nvidia soared 31%, adding a record $952 billion in market value.
The episode reaffirmed Wall Street's belief in the so-called Trump put. While the president didn't create Monday's rebound, his intervention helped supercharge it. It was the latest sign that Washington is increasingly willing to step in during moments of market peril — a pattern stretching back to the Biden administration.
Since the pandemic, investors have learned that Washington has a low tolerance for severe market pain.
Under Biden, the support mostly worked indirectly. He oversaw fiscal stimulus, industrial policy, and government spending that helped fuel growth, corporate profits, and eventually asset prices.
Trump's version has been more, let's say, direct.
When the bond market revolted after Liberation Day in 2025, he paused tariffs. Throughout the Iran war, the Trump administration has often shifted course when oil prices or bond yields moved too sharply. This time, he publicly backed Huang's return and offered Nvidia's board a national-security assurance.
That doesn't mean every sell-off or market dislocation gets erased. But when market stress becomes economically or politically consequential, investors have reason to believe Washington will try to contain the damage.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
What's different under Trump is that he's no longer merely setting the conditions for a rally. He's helping shape its catalysts — and becoming one himself — a theme BI has been covering throughout the year in our ""Future of Capitalism"" series.
Nvidia was already leading Monday's market rebound before the announcement of Huang's return. But Trump's intervention helped turn it into a historic market event.
That drives home the high stakes around Nvidia's share performance — not just as a standalone stock, but as the focal point of the AI trade. The company is a major index heavyweight, a core retirement-account holding, and a central supplier to the AI buildout.
The speed with which Nvidia's slide became everyone's problem helps explain why Washington stepped in. Less central stocks may never get the same treatment, but the possibility that an administration might intervene at all is a new variable for investors to weigh.
But political reassurance can only do so much. The questions that hit AI-trade sentiment last week remain: gigantic spending commitments, mounting debt, uncertain ROI, and lingering governance uncertainty at Nvidia.
The Trump put may be able to calm a panic, but it can't reassure investors about the AI trade's longer-term promises.
Today's trading will test whether Monday marked a durable reset or simply a knee-jerk relief rally. Either way, investors learned that when the heat is on, Washington is unlikely to remain a passive observer.
Your guide to what's moving markets

85,2026-11-24,"Korean camping gear specialist Kovea has recently launched one of the coolest little camp stoves on the market. The single-burner packs into a shiny-but-unassuming steel box that looks like a compact, rugged carry case for something valuable like a wristwatch or small camera. But what's really in there is a self-encased stove that emerges with a few folds to become a stable cook station at camp. 
Kovea is a name we don't get to call out often enough, but whenever we do, it's for a piece of highly thoughtful, innovative camping gear. Years before brands like Newr or Lectric Boil began developing battery-powered portable induction stoves for activities like boating and camping, Kovea was showing an even sleeker induction prototype around the outdoor expo circuit. It never produced a production version, but just the fact it was confidently exploring those waters nearly a decade before others was impressive in itself. 
Couple that with innovative retail products like its dual-fuel Twin Slim Lite stove, which remains one of the sleekest dual-burner portable camping stoves on the market, and you have a brand that knows how to put together clever cooking gear. 
The latest Kovea product to truly impress is the Container Stove, a tricky little contraption-in-a-box comprising a rugged stainless steel storage case that unpacks into a stable stove in a matter of seconds. We're not even sure it's all that practical compared to slim dual-burner stoves with more cooking space and power, but it's such a cool piece of design work, we want one anyway. Plus it'll squeeze into the corner of a Front Runner Wolf Pack Pro like few other stoves out there. 
If you're wondering why it's called the ""Container Stove"" rather than the more obvious ""Cube Stove,"" it's because Kovea already used the latter name long ago for this baby: 
 ""Cubic""? No go because that's tied up in this cute, little portable heater design: 
So ""Container Stove"" it is, and that name actually works a little better in conveying the idea that the device is both a stove and its own storage unit. When all folded up into box form and ready to travel, the little steel box measures just 4.9 x 3.2 x 3.2 inches (12.5 x 8.2 x 8.2 cm). So that tiny, palmable size isn't photographic trickery or giant hands ... the stove really is that small. 
To open, the top half of the box folds backward 90 degrees to reveal the folded-up burner, which folds out 90 degrees in the opposite direction into standing form. The four pot holders then rotate out into position to create a sizable cooking burner ready to comfortably hold small camping pots and pans. 
The top of the box then snaps back closed to create the form shown below. Drop open the rear hatch and you can slide the butane bottle in to power it up. 
The 8,700-BTU central burner features a concave design to help with wind protection. Kovea keeps the height of the pot holders down so the pot sits lower for better contact and less heat loss. It estimates boil time for a liter of water at just over three minutes.
The Container Stove weighs in at 1.4 lb (630 g) and the included carry sack adds 3.8 oz (109 g), but given how neat that packed box is, we'd be inclined to carry it without the sack. It's clearly not designed for backpacking or other ultralight backcountry endeavors, but it looks ideal for compact car camping kits or for use as a secondary burner in more elaborate kitchen setups.
As an example of the latter, I like to carry a coffee box as part of my camping kit, particularly for short overnighters when I'm moving on the next morning. I keep that box on top of the rest of the gear so it's easy to make coffee in the morning without having to remove a bunch of gear and rummage through multiple boxes before putting everything back in place and strapping it down all over again. 
Along with a French press, coffee grinder, coffee beans, tumblers and a few other pieces of kit, I store a small Optimus backpacking stove set in the coffee box. This way, I don't have to pull out the large dual-burner stove that's often packed further down. 
The Container Stove could potentially make an even better solution – stowing more neatly in a box corner than the round pot of the Optimus system and offering more stability than a canister-top stove/pot setup. Surprisingly, though, Optimus' current Crux Lite Solo cooking system has a higher listed output of 12,000 BTU and a comparable 3-minute/1-L boil time. The entire system measures 5.4 x 4.2 in (13.6 x 10.7, H x diameter), so it's quite comparable in size, but it also stores its own fuel canister inside that outer pot, whereas the Container Stove doesn't include a pot or canister, which store separately. 
So if I had to do it all over again, the Crux system would still be the way to go. But I'd be very tempted by the Container Stove just for its cool self-packing design and stainless steel look. I guess I could always bump the Optimus system over to a backpacking/bikpeacking setup, then use the Container Stove as part of my permanent car camping setup – very tempting so not counting that out. 
Kovea launched the Container Stove in South Korea two years ago, and it looks like it quietly brought it to the North American market earlier this year. Pricing varies, as the stainless model lists for US$158 on Kovea's Amazon store but only $103 at Big Tent Outdoors. The black version costs extra. 
If you like the idea of a self-encased hideaway stove, the $100 Snow Peak Home & Camp is another intriguing option. 
Take a look at Kovea's video clip to watch the Container Stove in action:
Source: Kovea

The controller gets high marks and can be used across a number of different products. Get it now for one of its best prices. 
If you've been using a generic third-party pad and noticed that button prompts feel wrong in certain titles, this is the fix. Microsoft's first-party controller displays correct Xbox button layouts across the Xbox app, Steam, Epic Games Store, and EA App.
No Bluetooth pairing dance, no driver installs, no wireless adapter. Plug the included 9-foot USB-C cable into a Windows PC and start playing immediately. When you want the freedom to play from the couch, switch to wireless and the same controller handles both seamlessly.
The sculpted surfaces and refined geometry are designed around how hands actually rest during long sessions, and the hybrid D-pad plus textured grips on the triggers, bumpers, and back case keep inputs precise without needing to grip hard. Battery life is another quiet win. 

Up to 40 hours of wireless play on standard AA batteries means you're not chasing a charging dock or worrying about a rechargeable pack losing capacity over time. When the batteries dip, plug in the included cable and keep playing without a pause.
The 3.5mm audio jack handles direct voice chat through any compatible headset, and the Xbox Accessories app lets you remap buttons and save profiles for different games if you want a more personalized setup.
This isn't the most exotic controller on the market, and it doesn't need to be. At $42.99 with the cable included, it's the lowest price in 90 days and a straightforward way to cover console, PC, and cloud gaming with one pad. If you've got a PC setup or an Xbox console and want a controller that just works across all of them, this is the one to grab while it's discounted."

89,2026-11-24,"
  By Steve Adams |
  at November 24, 2026 2:39pm CDT  
The Nationals are among the clubs showing interest in free agent infielder Jazz Chisholm Jr., reports Robert Murray of FanSided. There’s no indication a deal is close. Broadly speaking, the market for pitching has moved considerably faster than the market for bats — particularly over the past 72 hours or so (though Teruaki Sato is a notable exception).
Chisholm, 28, is coming off a down season by his standards, although in his case, that still means a 19-homer, 41-steal campaign. The lefty-swinging slugger’s strikeout rate trended in the wrong direction, climbing to nearly 30%, which weighed down his rate stats and left him with a roughly league-average .231/.312/.399 batting line. Defensive metrics, which can vary wildly from season-to-season but tend to offer a reasonably accurate perspective in larger samples, were all over the place in 2026 (-6 DRS, +13 Outs Above Average). Broadly speaking, Defensive Runs Saved has pegged Chisholm as a roughly average second baseman over the past few years, whereas Statcast’s Outs Above Average has him considerably above that threshold.
The fit with the Nationals would register as something of a surprise. Even on the back of a “down” year, Chisholm should have substantial earning power. The Nationals have generally spent at bottom-of-the-scale levels in recent offseasons as an increasingly lengthy rebuild continues to play out slower than hoped. As MLBTR’s Contract Tracker shows, they’ve only given out three multi-year deals in the past half decade — all $14MM or less in total — and have only once gone to an annual value north of $9MM: Nelson Cruz’s one-year, $15MM deal back in 2022. Chisholm would is in a tier of free agents that the Nats simply haven’t touched in recent memory.
Exactly where Chisholm would play in D.C. is an open question. The Nationals began to get longtime shortstop CJ Abrams some work at second base late in the 2026 season, recognizing that Abrams’ glovework at shortstop has graded among the worst in the league. Chisholm himself would be a stretch to return to shortstop. He’s reportedly signaled a willingness to play in the infield and/or in the outfield, which helps his case, but it’s been two years since he played center field for the Marlins. The Nats don’t have a set option at third base, so Chisholm could fit there if Washington brass isn’t concerned with the idea of blocking Brady House. It’s also at least possible that the Nats could get a massive offer for Abrams, who’s down to two years of club control remaining, but they’ve signaled a desire to hang onto him after a career-best performance in 2026.
As things stand, MLBTR contributor Ethan Hullihen has the Nationals projected for far and away the lowest payroll in baseball, at just over $52MM. That’s nearly $22MM behind the 29th-ranked Cardinals and $27MM behind the 28th-ranked Marlins. They’re obviously going to spend some money this winter, whether via signing free agents, acquiring contracts via trade, or a combination of the two. Washington has also hammered out extensions for starter Cade Cavalli and for center fielder Jacob Young over the past three-plus months, further signaling some willingness to spend.
Chisholm has thus far drawn reported interest from the Blue Jays and Padres. The Twins are known to be looking for help at second or first base. The Pirates have a potential opening at second base, depending on what happens with free agent Brandon Lowe, who rejected a $23.15MM qualifying offer from Pittsburgh. The Blue Jays, Angels, A’s and Rays are just a few of the teams that could look to add at second base, and Chisholm could draw interest from a far broader range of teams if he’s willing to play other positions and considered a viable option at said positions in the eyes of a few more clubs.
